- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### LogTinyLLM: Tiny Large Language Models Based Contextual Log Anomaly Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11071v1)
- **Authors**: Isaiah Thompson Ocansey, Ritwik Bhattacharya, Tanmay Sen
- **Abstract**: Log anomaly detection using traditional rule based or deep learning based methods is often challenging due to the large volume and highly complex nature of log sequence. So effective way of detection of anomalous sequence of logs is crucial for system maintenance and development. This paper proposes parameter efficient finetuning specifically low rank adaptation (LoRA) and adapter based approaches for finding contextual anomalies in sequence of logs in large log data set. It compares different tiny large language models (LLMs) on the Thunderbird dataset. The results show that LoRA based finetuning provides substantial performance improvements of 18 to 19 percentage over LogBert based full finetuning approach, achieving accuracy scores between 97.76% and 98.83% compared to 79.37%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LogTinyLLM: Tiny Large Language Models Based Contextual Log Anomaly Detection" investigates the use of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods, specifically LoRA and Adapter-based approaches, with tiny large language models (LLMs) for contextual log anomaly detection. It compares different tiny LLMs (OPT-1.3B, Phi-1.5, TinyLlama-1.1B, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B) on the Thunderbird dataset and demonstrates that LoRA-based fine-tuning provides significant performance improvements (around 18-19 percentage points increase in accuracy compared to full fine-tuning of LogBert). The study highlights the potential for lightweight, resource-efficient solutions for log anomaly detection using PEFT and tiny LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Relevance:** Log anomaly detection is a crucial task for system maintenance and security, making this work highly relevant.
    * **Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning:**  Focusing on PEFT methods addresses a key challenge in applying LLMs – their computational cost. Using tiny LLMs further reduces the computational requirements.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough empirical evaluation on a real-world log dataset (Thunderbird), comparing several tiny LLMs and PEFT configurations. The performance gains are well-documented.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper clearly explains the methodology, experimental setup, and results. The LoRA and adapter implementation details are adequate.
    * **Comparative analysis:** Comparing LoRA to traditional fine tuning methods is good practice, and shows strong improvements.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty:** The core idea of applying LoRA to LLMs is not novel *per se*. LoRA has been used in many NLP tasks and the application in log anomaly detection is a fairly straightforward adaptation. The "tiny LLM" aspect does reduce the scale, but this is also an incremental improvement rather than a fundamental innovation.
    * **Dataset Limitations:** While Thunderbird is a real-world dataset, it's important to acknowledge any potential biases or limitations inherent in the data. Generalizability to other types of log data needs to be addressed (although this is an inherent limitation of much research in this area).
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper primarily presents empirical results.  A more in-depth theoretical analysis of why certain LoRA configurations perform better or the impact of tiny LLM properties on performance would strengthen the work.
    * **Limited Exploration of Adapter Configurations:** The paper lacks comprehensive information regarding implementation of adapters.
    * **Baseline Selection:** While LogBert is a relevant baseline, comparing against more recent state-of-the-art log anomaly detection methods (especially other PEFT approaches) would be beneficial.
    * **Discussion of Limitations:** The paper could benefit from a more explicit discussion of the limitations of the proposed approach, such as potential challenges in applying it to different types of log data or the sensitivity to hyperparameter settings.

* **Significance:**

The significance of this paper lies in its practical demonstration of how PEFT techniques can be effectively utilized to deploy LLMs for log anomaly detection in resource-constrained environments. It helps to make LLM technology more accessible for operational tasks. However, because it is mostly empirical and lacks strong theoretical contributions, its impact on the long-term research direction may be limited.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates clear improvements in log anomaly detection performance using PEFT and tiny LLMs. However, the approach is not radically novel, mainly being an application of existing methods. The empirical evaluation is solid, but the lack of theoretical insight and limited exploration into adapter implementation keeps this from being a groundbreaking work. It's a useful application with a clear practical benefit, but it's not a fundamental advance in the field.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Function-to-Style Guidance of LLMs for Code Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11083v1)
- **Authors**: Longhui Zhang, Bin Wang, Jiahao Wang, Xiaofeng Zhao, Min Zhang, Hao Yang, Meishan Zhang, Yu Li, Jing Li, Jun Yu, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have made significant strides in code translation tasks. However, ensuring both the correctness and readability of translated code remains a challenge, limiting their effective adoption in real-world software development. In this work, we propose F2STrans, a function-to-style guiding paradigm designed to progressively improve the performance of LLMs in code translation. Our approach comprises two key stages: (1) Functional learning, which optimizes translation correctness using high-quality source-target code pairs mined from online programming platforms, and (2) Style learning, which improves translation readability by incorporating both positive and negative style examples. Additionally, we introduce a novel code translation benchmark that includes up-to-date source code, extensive test cases, and manually annotated ground-truth translations, enabling comprehensive functional and stylistic evaluations. Experiments on both our new benchmark and existing datasets demonstrate that our approach significantly improves code translation performance. Notably, our approach enables Qwen-1.5B to outperform prompt-enhanced Qwen-32B and GPT-4 on average across 20 diverse code translation scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Function-to-Style Guidance of LLMs for Code Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces F2STRANS, a two-stage framework designed to improve the correctness and readability of code translations produced by Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework consists of:

1.  **Functional Learning:** Fine-tunes an LLM using high-quality source-target code pairs mined from online programming platforms, selected based on solution consistency and differential testing, aiming to ensure functional correctness.
2.  **Style Learning:** Further refines the model by incorporating positive and negative style examples, encouraging the LLM to preserve the stylistic features of the source code in the translated target code, thus enhancing readability.  A new code translation benchmark is also introduced with updated source code, extensive test cases, and human-annotated ground-truth translations. Experiments on the new and existing benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach, showcasing that F2STRANS can improve code translation performance of LLMs and even enable smaller models to outperform larger, conventionally prompted models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Real Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial limitation of LLMs in code translation – ensuring both correctness and readability, which is vital for real-world software development.
    *   **Two-Stage Approach:** The separation of functional and stylistic learning is a logical and effective strategy. It allows the model to focus on correctness first, then refine the output for readability.  The use of both positive and negative stylistic examples is innovative and likely contributes to improved readability.
    *   **Data Curation:** The meticulous process of mining and selecting high-quality code pairs based on relevance and functional consistency is a strength, mitigating potential ambiguities and inconsistencies in online data. The introduction of a new benchmark addresses the limitations of existing datasets in terms of recent code, extensive test coverage, and human-annotated translations.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The comprehensive experiments on multiple LLMs and translation scenarios provides strong evidence for the effectiveness and generalizability of the proposed approach.  The comparison against strong baselines (Qwen, GPT-4) and various prompting techniques further strengthens the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on LLM Judge:** The reliance on another LLM (Qwen7B) to assess solution consistency introduces a potential bias.  The performance is contingent on the judge's ability to accurately assess the code, which may not be perfect.
    *   **Style Similarity Metric:** The CSSim metric, used for stylistic similarity, is limited to code pairs within the same language. While the authors explain this choice, it still restricts the ability to evaluate style comprehensively across different languages. CSSim and similar metrics aren't capturing all the subtleties of code style, which could be improved.
    *   **Generality of Style Transfer:**  While style is addressed, the paper could benefit from explicitly discussing the diversity of stylistic preferences among programmers. The current approach assumes a relatively uniform notion of desirable style, which might not hold in all scenarios. The study could investigate if the style is only useful for smaller models and how it translates to different LLM architectures.
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The paper's contributions, while practical, are incremental in the context of LLM fine-tuning. Functional fine-tuning and style transfer using LLMs have been studied before. The main novelty is in the specific approach for data curation, combining both functional and stylistic constraints.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work contributes practical techniques to improve code translation using LLMs, making them more useful for real-world software engineering tasks. The new benchmark fills an important gap in evaluation resources. The results demonstrate that targeted fine-tuning can significantly enhance the performance of smaller models, making them competitive with, or even better than, larger, more expensive models. The study could impact developers that are trying to reduce computational overhead with minimal performance drops.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses an important problem, presents a well-defined and executed approach, and provides strong experimental evidence. The data curation and the separation between functional and stylistic learning add value. However, the limited novelty in the high-level approach and reliance on existing methods diminish the significance. The work's practical impact is likely to be significant, providing useful techniques for leveraging LLMs for code translation. Given these strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Traditional Algorithms: Leveraging LLMs for Accurate Cross-Border Entity Identification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11086v1)
- **Authors**: Andres Azqueta-Gavaldón, Joaquin Ramos Cosgrove
- **Abstract**: The growing prevalence of cross-border financial activities in global markets has underscored the necessity of accurately identifying and classifying foreign entities. This practice is essential within the Spanish financial system for ensuring robust risk management, regulatory adherence, and the prevention of financial misconduct. This process involves a labor-intensive entity-matching task, where entities need to be validated against available reference sources. Challenges arise from linguistic variations, special characters, outdated names, and changes in legal forms, complicating traditional matching algorithms like Jaccard, cosine, and Levenshtein distances. These methods struggle with contextual nuances and semantic relationships, leading to mismatches. To address these limitations, we explore Large Language Models (LLMs) as a flexible alternative. LLMs leverage extensive training to interpret context, handle abbreviations, and adapt to legal transitions. We evaluate traditional methods, Hugging Face-based LLMs, and interface-based LLMs (e.g., Microsoft Copilot, Alibaba's Qwen 2.5) using a dataset of 65 Portuguese company cases. Results show traditional methods achieve accuracies over 92% but suffer high false positive rates (20-40%). Interface-based LLMs outperform, achieving accuracies above 93%, F1 scores exceeding 96%, and lower false positives (40-80%).
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on a rigorous analysis of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond Traditional Algorithms: Leveraging LLMs for Accurate Cross-Border Entity Identification" explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) for improving the accuracy of cross-border entity identification, a crucial task in financial regulatory compliance. It argues that traditional methods like Jaccard, cosine, and Levenshtein distances struggle with linguistic variations, abbreviations, and legal form changes. The study compares the performance of traditional algorithms, Hugging Face-based LLMs, and interface-based LLMs (Microsoft Copilot, Alibaba's Qwen 2.5) on a dataset of Portuguese company cases. The results demonstrate that while traditional methods have high accuracy, they suffer from high false positive rates. Interface-based LLMs show superior performance, achieving higher accuracy, F1 scores, and lower false positive rates. The paper concludes that LLMs hold promise for enhancing entity matching by improving accuracy and efficiency while minimizing human intervention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core strength lies in its empirical comparison of different LLM approaches (Hugging Face-based vs. interface-based) with established distance-based methods in a *specific and practically relevant domain*: cross-border entity identification in the financial sector.  While the idea of using LLMs for entity matching isn't entirely novel in general (as the literature review indicates), the paper's specific focus on *regulatory compliance*, its use of *interface-based LLMs* (which are less explored than the API-based approaches), and its performance analysis with a particular attention to reducing *false positives (FPR)* in financial datasets contribute a level of nuance that adds novelty. The emphasis on lowering FPR is particularly important in high-stakes applications.

*Weaknesses*: The dataset's size (65 cases) is a significant limitation. While well-defined, it's small and may not be representative of the full complexity encountered in real-world scenarios. The fine-tuning process is not clearly explained or justified.  The paper does not delve deeply into the *architectural choices or pre-training data* of the LLMs used. This makes it difficult to generalize the findings or understand *why* interface-based LLMs perform better. A more detailed analysis of the types of errors made by each approach (error analysis) would significantly strengthen the conclusions.

*Significance*: The paper has practical significance. Accurate entity identification is vital for risk management, regulatory compliance, and fraud prevention. Reducing false positives is particularly important because incorrectly classifying an entity could result in serious compliance violations.  The comparative analysis provides valuable insights for practitioners seeking to adopt LLM-based solutions for entity matching.  However, given the dataset limitations, the study serves more as a *proof-of-concept* and a starting point for future research.

*Potential Influence*: The paper could influence the adoption of LLMs in financial institutions, especially for tasks requiring high accuracy and low error rates. The focus on interface-based LLMs is also potentially influential, as these interfaces often lower the barrier to entry for organizations with limited technical expertise. The identification of areas where LLMs still struggle (abbreviations, lexical variations) provides directions for future model development and fine-tuning.

Justification for Score:

The paper demonstrates incremental novelty due to the specific problem context, the consideration of interface-based LLMs, and the focus on minimizing FPRs. Its practical relevance in the financial domain adds to its significance. However, the small dataset, lack of detailed model analysis, and limited generalizability prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. It doesn't introduce fundamentally new methods but rather applies existing LLMs to a relevant problem, showcasing specific advantages and disadvantages.  The absence of a more elaborate fine-tuning strategy for LLMs limits the novelty of the approach.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Devil behind the mask: An emergent safety vulnerability of Diffusion LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11097v1)
- **Authors**: Zichen Wen, Jiashu Qu, Dongrui Liu, Zhiyuan Liu, Ruixi Wu, Yicun Yang, Xiangqi Jin, Haoyun Xu, Xuyang Liu, Weijia Li, Chaochao Lu, Jing Shao, Conghui He, Linfeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) have recently emerged as a powerful alternative to autoregressive LLMs, offering faster inference and greater interactivity via parallel decoding and bidirectional modeling. However, despite strong performance in code generation and text infilling, we identify a fundamental safety concern: existing alignment mechanisms fail to safeguard dLLMs against context-aware, masked-input adversarial prompts, exposing novel vulnerabilities. To this end, we present DIJA, the first systematic study and jailbreak attack framework that exploits unique safety weaknesses of dLLMs. Specifically, our proposed DIJA constructs adversarial interleaved mask-text prompts that exploit the text generation mechanisms of dLLMs, i.e., bidirectional modeling and parallel decoding. Bidirectional modeling drives the model to produce contextually consistent outputs for masked spans, even when harmful, while parallel decoding limits model dynamic filtering and rejection sampling of unsafe content. This causes standard alignment mechanisms to fail, enabling harmful completions in alignment-tuned dLLMs, even when harmful behaviors or unsafe instructions are directly exposed in the prompt. Through comprehensive experiments, we demonstrate that DIJA significantly outperforms existing jailbreak methods, exposing a previously overlooked threat surface in dLLM architectures. Notably, our method achieves up to 100% keyword-based ASR on Dream-Instruct, surpassing the strongest prior baseline, ReNeLLM, by up to 78.5% in evaluator-based ASR on JailbreakBench and by 37.7 points in StrongREJECT score, while requiring no rewriting or hiding of harmful content in the jailbreak prompt. Our findings underscore the urgent need for rethinking safety alignment in this emerging class of language models. Code is available at https://github.com/ZichenWen1/DIJA.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper, titled "The Devil Behind the Mask: An Emergent Safety Vulnerability of Diffusion LLMs," identifies a novel safety vulnerability in diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs).  Unlike autoregressive LLMs, dLLMs leverage bidirectional context modeling and parallel decoding, leading to faster inference and increased interactivity. However, the authors demonstrate that these architectural differences make dLLMs susceptible to context-aware, masked-input adversarial prompts. They introduce DIJA, a jailbreak attack framework that exploits these weaknesses by constructing adversarial interleaved mask-text prompts. The DIJA framework leverages bidirectional modeling to drive the model to produce contextually consistent outputs (even if harmful) for masked spans, while parallel decoding limits dynamic filtering of unsafe content.  Experiments demonstrate that DIJA significantly outperforms existing jailbreak methods on various dLLMs across multiple jailbreak benchmarks, often achieving near-perfect attack success rates without rewriting or hiding harmful content. The paper argues that existing safety alignment mechanisms fail to protect dLLMs from these attacks and highlights the urgent need for rethinking safety alignment strategies.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The core novelty of this paper is the identification and systematic exploitation of a previously unaddressed vulnerability in dLLMs. While jailbreak attacks on LLMs are a well-studied area, the focus has predominantly been on autoregressive models. The authors effectively demonstrate how the unique architecture of dLLMs (bidirectional modeling and parallel decoding) creates a distinct attack surface. The construction of adversarial prompts using interleaved mask-text is also a novel approach, specifically tailored to exploit these architectural features. The DIJA framework provides a method that generates harmful instructions, not by directly inserting bad content into the prompt, but by asking the LLM to generate the harmful content, which is different from current attacks that rely on some transformation to the initial prompt.

**Significance:** This paper is significant because dLLMs are gaining increasing prominence due to their efficiency and interactivity.  The discovery of a fundamental safety vulnerability in this emerging class of models has important implications for their deployment.  If left unaddressed, these vulnerabilities could be exploited to generate harmful content, bypassing existing safety measures. The DIJA framework provides a concrete tool to evaluate and improve the robustness of dLLMs. The paper strongly argues for and makes clear that current methods of ensuring safety may need to be rethought due to this new avenue for harmful behavior. Furthermore, the demonstration of high attack success rates, even on models that have been fine-tuned for safety, underscores the urgency of the issue.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and explains the vulnerability in dLLMs.
*   **Effective Methodology:** The DIJA framework is well-designed and effective in exploiting the vulnerability.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of DIJA and the weakness of existing defenses.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for the development and deployment of dLLMs.
*   **Well-written and organized:** Paper is clear and well-written, easily conveys information to the reader.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Variety:** While the paper evaluates several dLLMs, the range of architectures could be expanded. The authors make explicit in the limitations section that due to the lack of models, an expanded evaluation could not be performed.
*   **Defense Evaluation Depth:** Although some baseline defenses are evaluated, a more in-depth analysis of the robustness of DIJA against advanced defenses would be beneficial.
*   **Reliance on Language Model for Prompt Refinement:** DIJA relies on a language model to generate the mask-text jailbreak prompts, which introduces a potential dependency and associated computational cost.

**Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the development of safety alignment strategies for dLLMs. It highlights the need to consider the unique architectural features of these models and to develop defenses that are specifically tailored to address their vulnerabilities. The DIJA framework can serve as a benchmark for evaluating the robustness of future dLLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM safety. The identification of a new vulnerability in dLLMs, coupled with the development of a practical attack framework, has important implications for the development and deployment of these models. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### KptLLM++: Towards Generic Keypoint Comprehension with Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11102v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Yang, Wang Zeng, Sheng Jin, Lumin Xu, Wentao Liu, Chen Qian, Zhen Li, Ruimao Zhang
- **Abstract**: The emergence of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has revolutionized image understanding by bridging textual and visual modalities. However, these models often struggle with capturing fine-grained semantic information, such as the precise identification and analysis of object keypoints. Keypoints, as structure-aware, pixel-level, and compact representations of objects, particularly articulated ones, play a crucial role in applications such as fine-grained image analysis, object retrieval, and behavior recognition. In this paper, we propose KptLLM++, a novel multimodal large language model that specifically designed for generic keypoint comprehension through the integration of diverse input modalities guided by user-defined instructions. By unifying keypoint detection across varied contexts, KptLLM++ establishes itself as an advanced interface, fostering more effective human-AI collaboration. The model is built upon a novel identify-then-detect paradigm, which first interprets keypoint semantics and subsequently localizes their precise positions through a structured chain-of-thought reasoning mechanism. To push the boundaries of performance, we have scaled up the training dataset to over 500K samples, encompassing diverse objects, keypoint categories, image styles, and scenarios with complex occlusions. This extensive scaling enables KptLLM++ to unlock its potential, achieving remarkable accuracy and generalization. Comprehensive experiments on multiple keypoint detection benchmarks demonstrate its state-of-the-art performance, underscoring its potential as a unified solution for fine-grained image understanding and its transformative implications for human-AI interaction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "KptLLM++: Towards Generic Keypoint Comprehension with Large Language Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KptLLM++, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) specifically designed for generic keypoint comprehension.  KptLLM++ aims to address the limitations of existing MLLMs, which often struggle with fine-grained semantic understanding, particularly in the context of object keypoints.  The model uses an "identify-then-detect" strategy, first interpreting keypoint semantics and then localizing their precise positions.  KptLLM++ integrates visual and textual modalities and is trained on a large dataset (>500K samples) covering diverse objects, keypoint categories, and scenarios. The paper claims state-of-the-art performance on multiple keypoint detection benchmarks. KptLLM++ has the ability to adapt to different user needs and interaction styles by accepting a variety of instructions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The "identify-then-detect" strategy, while not entirely new in the broader computer vision literature, is a well-motivated approach in the context of MLLMs. The integration of visual and textual information for keypoint understanding is a natural extension of existing MLLM capabilities, but KptLLM++ does provide a unified framework. Scaling up the training dataset to >500K samples is a significant practical contribution but doesn't necessarily represent groundbreaking conceptual innovation. The concept of unifying keypoint detection across different task scenarios like Keypoint Semantic Understanding, Visual Prompt-based Keypoint Detection, and Textual Prompt-based Keypoint Detection is solid and adds value. However, the individual tasks themselves are based on existing research (i.e. POMNet, CLAMP).

*   **Significance:** Keypoint detection is a fundamental problem in computer vision, and MLLMs have the potential to improve its performance. If KptLLM++ demonstrably enhances human-AI interaction and provides a more versatile and user-adaptive keypoint detection process, it would be significant.  The large-scale training dataset is valuable and makes the work reproducible for future researchers. The claim of state-of-the-art performance on multiple keypoint detection benchmarks requires a careful look at the experimental details and how fair comparisons are being made, particularly since other models might not be designed specifically for the 'generic' setup.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The unification of different keypoint understanding tasks within a single MLLM is a strong point.
    *   The large-scale dataset provides a valuable resource for the community.
    *   The paper provides evidence that KptLLM++ can handle complex scenarios.
    *   The model's design, specifically using both visual and textual prompts, makes it user-friendly.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty is incremental, building upon existing MLLM architectures and keypoint detection strategies. The identify-then-detect paradigm is not particularly groundbreaking.
    *   The experiments are limited to GT bounding boxes which artificially improves performance compared to methods that must perform object detection.
    *   The improvements achieved in performance over existing approaches might be driven primarily by the scale of the training data, as opposed to architectural innovations. The benefits derived from the ItD strategy and prompt feature extractor may not provide enough novel contribution.
    *   The "state-of-the-art" claim should be scrutinized, considering the specific experimental setup (GT boxes) and potentially the model architectures being compared.
    *   The lack of a qualitative error analysis section to address the limitations of the model is a significant omission.
    *   There is limited analysis of the cost of training and inference for KptLLM++ compared to simpler methods.
    *   There are sections of the text where the wording is vague and requires clarification such as "the paper provides evidence that KptLLM++ can handle complex scenarios."

*   **Potential Influence:** The work has the potential to inspire future research in fine-grained visual understanding with MLLMs. The code, model weights, and large-scale data will be valuable to the community. If the performance claims hold up, it could push the field toward more versatile and user-adaptive keypoint detection systems.
    *   Provides inspiration to other research in fine-grained understanding with MLLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6/10**.

Rationale: KptLLM++ presents a solid and well-engineered extension of existing MLLM capabilities toward a specific and important computer vision problem. The unification of different keypoint-related tasks and the effort to create a large-scale training dataset are valuable contributions. However, the conceptual novelty is somewhat limited, with the core idea relying on the established "identify-then-detect" framework and incremental architectural improvements. The dependence of a large amount of the experiments on GT bounding boxes weakens the state-of-the-art claim and the overall impact of the paper. The model has good potential for influencing future research within the field but has limited theoretical significance. Overall, the work represents a valuable engineering effort with incremental scientific innovation.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multi-Trigger Poisoning Amplifies Backdoor Vulnerabilities in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11112v1)
- **Authors**: Sanhanat Sivapiromrat, Caiqi Zhang, Marco Basaldella, Nigel Collier
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have shown that Large Language Models (LLMs) are vulnerable to data poisoning attacks, where malicious training examples embed hidden behaviours triggered by specific input patterns. However, most existing works assume a phrase and focus on the attack's effectiveness, offering limited understanding of trigger mechanisms and how multiple triggers interact within the model. In this paper, we present a framework for studying poisoning in LLMs. We show that multiple distinct backdoor triggers can coexist within a single model without interfering with each other, enabling adversaries to embed several triggers concurrently. Using multiple triggers with high embedding similarity, we demonstrate that poisoned triggers can achieve robust activation even when tokens are substituted or separated by long token spans. Our findings expose a broader and more persistent vulnerability surface in LLMs. To mitigate this threat, we propose a post hoc recovery method that selectively retrains specific model components based on a layer-wise weight difference analysis. Our method effectively removes the trigger behaviour with minimal parameter updates, presenting a practical and efficient defence against multi-trigger poisoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores multi-trigger poisoning attacks in Large Language Models (LLMs). It demonstrates that multiple distinct backdoor triggers can coexist within a single model without interfering with each other. Furthermore, it shows that triggers with high embedding similarity can achieve robust activation, even when tokens are substituted or separated by long token spans. The paper also proposes a post-hoc recovery method that selectively retrains specific model components based on a layer-wise weight difference analysis to mitigate this threat. This method effectively removes trigger behavior with minimal parameter updates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is in extending the data poisoning attack paradigm to encompass multiple triggers within LLMs. While single-trigger poisoning attacks have been explored before, this paper provides a framework to understand and analyze multi-trigger interactions. It goes beyond simple coexistence and investigates how embedding similarity and token separation influence the effectiveness of such attacks. The proposed defense mechanism, selective retraining based on weight differences, offers a practical and efficient approach to mitigating these vulnerabilities.

*   **Significance:** The findings expose a broader and more persistent vulnerability surface in LLMs. By demonstrating that multiple triggers can coexist and even reinforce each other, the paper highlights a significant security risk that was previously under-explored. The proposed defense mechanism is a valuable contribution as it offers a way to recover poisoned models without requiring full retraining, making it a more practical solution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of multi-trigger poisoning and outlines the research questions.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** It provides a thorough analysis of trigger interactions, including the impact of embedding similarity and token separation.
    *   **Effective Mitigation Strategy:** The proposed selective retraining method is both effective and efficient.
    *   **Strong Experimental Setup:** The experiments are well-designed and use established datasets and models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dirty-Label Focus:** The study focuses on a dirty-label poisoning attack, which, while effective, might be easier to detect than more subtle clean-label attacks.
    *   **Model Size Limitation:** Experiments were performed on smaller LLMs (2-3B). While the authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest that the findings should extend to larger models, empirical validation on larger models would further strengthen the results.
    *   **Limited Trigger Types:** The experiments primarily use two-token trigger phrases focused on named entities. Exploring more complex and diverse trigger types could add to the paper's robustness.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM security and defense. It highlights the importance of considering multi-trigger interactions when evaluating the robustness of LLMs against data poisoning attacks. The proposed defense mechanism could be adopted and further refined by other researchers and practitioners.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** Although the paper has some weaknesses, its overall contribution is significant. The exploration of multi-trigger poisoning expands the understanding of potential attack surfaces in LLMs. The framework, analysis, and post-hoc defense mechanisms are all valuable contributions to the research community. The selective retraining strategy represents a pragmatic approach to model recovery that should be welcomed by the research community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**
The paper is well-structured, offers valuable insights into multi-trigger poisoning, and proposes a practical mitigation strategy. However, the focus on dirty-label attacks, limitations of the models tested, and trigger types explored limit the paper to a 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MSA at ImageCLEF 2025 Multimodal Reasoning: Multilingual Multimodal Reasoning With Ensemble Vision Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11114v1)
- **Authors**: Seif Ahmed, Mohamed T. Younes, Abdelrahman Moustafa, Abdelrahman Allam, Hamza Moustafa
- **Abstract**: We present a robust ensemble-based system for multilingual multimodal reasoning, designed for the ImageCLEF 2025 EXAMS V challenge. Our approach integrates Gemini 2.5 Flash for visual description, Gemini 1.5 Pro for caption refinement and consistency checks, and Gemini 2.5 Pro as a reasoner which handles final answer selection, all coordinated through carefully engineered few-shot and zero-shot prompts. We conducted an extensive ablation study, training several large language models (Gemini 2.5 Flash, Phi 4, Gemma 3, Mistral) on an English dataset and its multilingual augmented version. Additionally, we evaluated Gemini 2.5 Flash in a zero-shot setting for comparison and found it to substantially outperform the trained models. Prompt design also proved critical: enforcing concise, language-normalized formats and prohibiting explanatory text boosted model accuracy on the English validation set from 55.9% to 61.7%. On the official leaderboard, our system (Team MSA) achieved first place overall in the multilingual track with 81.4% accuracy, and led 11 out of 13 individual language tracks, with top results such as 95.07% for Croatian and 92.12% for Italian. These findings highlight that lightweight OCR-VLM ensembles, when paired with precise prompt strategies and cross-lingual augmentation, can outperform heavier end-to-end models in high-stakes, multilingual educational settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a multimodal reasoning system designed for the ImageCLEF 2025 EXAMS-V challenge, focusing on multilingual visual question answering. The system uses an ensemble approach combining Gemini 2.5 Flash for visual description, Gemini 1.5 Pro for caption refinement, and Gemini 2.5 Pro for reasoning and answer selection. The authors conduct an ablation study, training and comparing several large language models on both English and multilingual augmented datasets. Key findings highlight the importance of prompt engineering (particularly the use of strict, concise prompts) and multilingual data augmentation for improved performance. The system achieved first place on the multilingual track of the challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its ensemble approach leveraging the strengths of different Gemini models within a specific prompt engineering framework for multilingual VQA. While the individual components (Gemini models, prompt engineering, data augmentation) are not novel in isolation, their specific combination and application to this specific challenge, with a careful ablation study, represents a valuable contribution. However, other approaches to tackle this issue of multimodal reasoning, like end-to-end training, might be more efficient and require less engineering than the approach provided here.
*   **Significance:** Achieving state-of-the-art performance on the ImageCLEF EXAMS-V challenge demonstrates the practical effectiveness of the proposed system. The paper also offers valuable insights into the impact of prompt engineering, model architecture selection, and multilingual data augmentation. The analysis of how different prompt strategies influence performance, especially across languages, is significant and can inform future research. The findings about the limitations of smaller models (e.g., Mistral) in benefiting from multilingual data are also valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive ablation study.
    *   Detailed explanation of the system architecture and design choices.
    *   Strong experimental results on a challenging multilingual benchmark.
    *   Valuable insights into the role of prompt engineering and data augmentation.
    *   Clarity of writing and presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on proprietary models (Gemini) limits reproducibility.
    *   The system is highly engineered and may not generalize well to other tasks or domains without significant modification.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough comparison with other state-of-the-art methods, including end-to-end approaches, and a discussion of the trade-offs between different approaches.
    *   The paper does not deeply investigate failure cases or error analysis, which would further strengthen the analysis.
    *   The reliance on a two-stage pipeline (OCR-VLM and Reasoner) adds unnecessary complexity.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the design of future multilingual VQA systems by demonstrating the effectiveness of ensemble methods, prompt engineering, and multilingual data augmentation. The insights gained from the ablation study can guide researchers in selecting appropriate model architectures and training strategies. The challenge results provide a valuable benchmark for comparing future systems.

**Justification:** The paper presents a well-executed study with significant empirical results. The careful design and extensive analysis of prompt engineering techniques, data augmentation strategies, and model selection make it a notable contribution in the field. While the reliance on proprietary models and the engineered nature of the system are limitations, the practical success and valuable insights justify a positive evaluation. However, the lack of thorough comparison with other state-of-the-art approaches and the engineered nature of the approach prevent a truly exceptional score. The significant experimental results and analysis make it a valuable piece for the community.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### What Should LLMs Forget? Quantifying Personal Data in LLMs for Right-to-Be-Forgotten Requests
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11128v1)
- **Authors**: Dimitri Staufer
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) can memorize and reveal personal information, raising concerns regarding compliance with the EU's GDPR, particularly the Right to Be Forgotten (RTBF). Existing machine unlearning methods assume the data to forget is already known but do not address how to identify which individual-fact associations are stored in the model. Privacy auditing techniques typically operate at the population level or target a small set of identifiers, limiting applicability to individual-level data inquiries. We introduce WikiMem, a dataset of over 5,000 natural language canaries covering 243 human-related properties from Wikidata, and a model-agnostic metric to quantify human-fact associations in LLMs. Our approach ranks ground-truth values against counterfactuals using calibrated negative log-likelihood across paraphrased prompts. We evaluate 200 individuals across 15 LLMs (410M-70B parameters), showing that memorization correlates with subject web presence and model scale. We provide a foundation for identifying memorized personal data in LLMs at the individual level, enabling the dynamic construction of forget sets for machine unlearning and RTBF requests.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of quantifying and identifying personal data memorized by Large Language Models (LLMs) for Right-to-Be-Forgotten (RTBF) requests under GDPR. It introduces WikiMem, a large-scale dataset of over 5,000 natural language "canaries" for probing LLMs about 243 human-related properties derived from Wikidata. The paper also presents a model-agnostic metric using calibrated negative log-likelihood (NLL) ranking over counterfactuals to quantify the association strength between individuals and facts. The study evaluates 15 LLMs (410M-70B parameters) and shows that memorization correlates with subject web presence and model scale. The authors aim to provide a foundation for identifying memorized personal data in LLMs at the individual level, enabling the dynamic construction of forget sets for machine unlearning and RTBF requests.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a valuable dataset (WikiMem) specifically designed to assess personal data memorization in LLMs.  The development of the model-agnostic metric based on calibrated NLL ranking is also a novel contribution, providing a means to quantify association strength independent of model architecture. The combination of the dataset and metric to perform a large-scale empirical study across various LLMs, including recent architectures like LLaMA and Mistral, is a significant advancement. Prior work either focuses on population-level leakage or uses limited sets of identifiers, while this paper attempts entity-level quantification.

*   **Significance:**  The paper tackles a critical problem concerning LLM compliance with privacy regulations like GDPR, especially RTBF. LLMs' capacity to memorize and potentially leak personal information poses significant legal and ethical challenges. The research provides a practical approach for identifying and quantifying this risk, potentially enabling more effective machine unlearning strategies. The finding that memorization correlates with web presence is also important, suggesting potential areas of focus for mitigation strategies. The paper's contributions have the potential to inform the development of privacy-preserving LLMs and assist in responding to RTBF requests.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Large-scale and diverse dataset:** WikiMem is a significant resource for evaluating LLM memorization of personal data.
    *   **Model-agnostic metric:** The calibrated NLL ranking makes the approach applicable to various LLM architectures.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The study covers a wide range of LLMs and individuals.
    *   **Practical implications:** The research provides a foundation for building systems to comply with RTBF requests.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Focus on Wikidata:**  While Wikidata provides structured knowledge, the reliance on its data may limit the scope to well-documented facts and introduce biases.  The paper acknowledges this limitation, but it's important to note that real-world personal data is often less structured and more nuanced.
    *   **Simplifying assumptions:** The study treats subject-property pairs as memorized if *all* template variants yield a rank-1 prediction. While strict, it might be too conservative. Furthermore, assuming linguistic diversity suffices to empirically show memorization is questionable. What if the dataset simply exhibits lexical bias?
    *   **Canary Construction Limitations:** The methodology hinges heavily on canary construction.  The effectiveness is likely tied to the prompt engineering used to generate them. It is possible that the language diversity doesn't generalize to real-world cases where adversarial inputs are used instead.
    *   **Evaluation Scope:** Limited focus on a few top properties. While this allows for deeper analysis, it would be useful to have insight on a wider range of properties.
    *   **Counterfactual Difficulty:** The paper acknowledges but doesn't address the potential variability in difficulty and distinctiveness across counterfactuals.
    *   **Lack of Downstream Task:** The research does not demonstrate the utility of its method in a downstream application such as machine unlearning. The usefulness is demonstrated on a theoretical and statistical level, but not in practice.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on LLM privacy, machine unlearning, and responsible AI development. It can provide a benchmark for evaluating the effectiveness of unlearning methods and inform the design of more privacy-preserving LLMs. The findings regarding the relationship between web presence and memorization can also guide mitigation strategies.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper represents a substantial contribution to the field of LLM privacy. It addresses a crucial problem with a novel dataset, a model-agnostic metric, and a comprehensive evaluation. While the study has some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The paper lays a strong foundation for future research on identifying and quantifying personal data in LLMs and enabling practical RTBF compliance.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hashed Watermark as a Filter: Defeating Forging and Overwriting Attacks in Weight-based Neural Network Watermarking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11137v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Yao, Jin Song, Jian Jin
- **Abstract**: As valuable digital assets, deep neural networks necessitate robust ownership protection, positioning neural network watermarking (NNW) as a promising solution. Among various NNW approaches, weight-based methods are favored for their simplicity and practicality; however, they remain vulnerable to forging and overwriting attacks. To address those challenges, we propose NeuralMark, a robust method built around a hashed watermark filter. Specifically, we utilize a hash function to generate an irreversible binary watermark from a secret key, which is then used as a filter to select the model parameters for embedding. This design cleverly intertwines the embedding parameters with the hashed watermark, providing a robust defense against both forging and overwriting attacks. An average pooling is also incorporated to resist fine-tuning and pruning attacks. Furthermore, it can be seamlessly integrated into various neural network architectures, ensuring broad applicability. Theoretically, we analyze its security boundary. Empirically, we verify its effectiveness and robustness across 13 distinct Convolutional and Transformer architectures, covering five image classification tasks and one text generation task. The source codes are available at https://github.com/AIResearch-Group/NeuralMark.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes NeuralMark, a white-box neural network watermarking (NNW) technique to protect model ownership. It addresses the vulnerability of existing weight-based methods to forging and overwriting attacks. NeuralMark introduces a "hashed watermark filter" that uses a hash function to generate an irreversible binary watermark from a secret key. This watermark is then used as a filter to select specific model parameters for watermark embedding. The core idea is to entangle the embedding parameters with the hashed watermark, making it difficult for attackers to reverse-engineer the watermark or overwrite it with a counterfeit. Average pooling is incorporated to enhance robustness against fine-tuning and pruning attacks. The paper presents a theoretical security analysis and validates NeuralMark's effectiveness through experiments on various architectures, datasets, and tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the use of a hashed watermark as a filter for selecting embedding parameters. This approach aims to provide a defense against both forging and overwriting attacks by obfuscating gradient calculation and isolating the embedding parameters, two areas where previous weight-based techniques have struggled. The average pooling adds some resilience to pruning and fine-tuning, but it is not particularly novel, since this technique has already been explored in NNW literature.

*   **Significance:** The ability to simultaneously defend against forging and overwriting attacks is significant. Addressing these vulnerabilities strengthens the practical value of weight-based NNW. The paper provides a clear theoretical analysis that provides a rigorous benchmark for watermark detection. Experimental results presented show a positive outcome, indicating that the proposed method resists the considered attacks to a significant extent. The authors made the code publicly available, which is commendable and contributes to the reproducibility and adoption of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined goals.
    *   The hashed watermark filter concept is innovative.
    *   Theoretical analysis offers insights into the security bounds.
    *   Extensive experiments across diverse architectures and datasets.
    *   Code availability promotes reproducibility.
    *   Good results in terms of resistance to forging and overwriting attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is somewhat limited. While the paper covers different datasets and architectures, further exploration is warranted on the security of NeuralMark against adaptive attacks. Would stronger adversaries be able to identify parameters related to the watermark or infer some information about the secret key?
    *   The performance of the watermark might degrade with more adversarial attacks, a trade-off that must be clearly stated in the document.
    *   Although average pooling provides robustness against pruning and fine-tuning, it is not a very novel contribution by itself.
    *   The practical complexity in terms of computational overheads should be more carefully considered.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   NeuralMark could become a practical solution for protecting model ownership.
    *   The concept of using a hashed watermark as a filter could inspire new NNW techniques.
    *   The security analysis provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating the robustness of other NNW methods.

*   **Overall Assessment:**
    The paper presents a significant improvement over previous weight-based NNW approaches by addressing the crucial vulnerabilities of forging and overwriting. While the average pooling and the experimental evaluation can be slightly expanded, the innovative hashed watermark filtering concept and theoretical analysis contribute significantly to the field of NNW.

Score: 8

**Rationale:**

The paper scores an 8 due to the combination of a novel approach (hashed watermark filter), clear problem statement, a theoretical analysis of the security guarantees, and strong experimental validation. The weaknesses mentioned prevent it from scoring higher, but the core idea is compelling and presents a valuable contribution to the NNW field. The improvement over existing weight-based methods in terms of forging and overwriting attack resistance justifies a high score, making this research impactful and potentially widely adopted.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Latent Space Consistency for Sparse-View CT Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11152v1)
- **Authors**: Duoyou Chen, Yunqing Chen, Can Zhang, Zhou Wang, Cheng Chen, Ruoxiu Xiao
- **Abstract**: Computed Tomography (CT) is a widely utilized imaging modality in clinical settings. Using densely acquired rotational X-ray arrays, CT can capture 3D spatial features. However, it is confronted with challenged such as significant time consumption and high radiation exposure. CT reconstruction methods based on sparse-view X-ray images have garnered substantial attention from researchers as they present a means to mitigate costs and risks. In recent years, diffusion models, particularly the Latent Diffusion Model (LDM), have demonstrated promising potential in the domain of 3D CT reconstruction. Nonetheless, due to the substantial differences between the 2D latent representation of X-ray modalities and the 3D latent representation of CT modalities, the vanilla LDM is incapable of achieving effective alignment within the latent space. To address this issue, we propose the Consistent Latent Space Diffusion Model (CLS-DM), which incorporates cross-modal feature contrastive learning to efficiently extract latent 3D information from 2D X-ray images and achieve latent space alignment between modalities. Experimental results indicate that CLS-DM outperforms classical and state-of-the-art generative models in terms of standard voxel-level metrics (PSNR, SSIM) on the LIDC-IDRI and CTSpine1K datasets. This methodology not only aids in enhancing the effectiveness and economic viability of sparse X-ray reconstructed CT but can also be generalized to other cross-modal transformation tasks, such as text-to-image synthesis. We have made our code publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CLS-DM-50D6/ to facilitate further research and applications in other domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method called Consistent Latent Space Diffusion Model (CLS-DM) for sparse-view CT reconstruction. It addresses the challenge of aligning the 2D latent space of X-ray images with the 3D latent space of CT scans in diffusion models. CLS-DM incorporates cross-modal feature contrastive learning to extract 3D information from 2D X-ray images, facilitating latent space alignment. An autoregressive component is used in conjunction with the contrastive learning to prevent feature representation degradation. Experimental results on the LIDC-IDRI and CTSpine1K datasets demonstrate that CLS-DM outperforms classical and state-of-the-art generative models in terms of PSNR and SSIM. The authors also mention that their code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty of the paper lies in the integration of contrastive learning within a latent diffusion model framework for sparse-view CT reconstruction. While contrastive learning and diffusion models are individually established techniques, their combination specifically to address the latent space misalignment issue in this context presents a novel approach.  The addition of the autoregressive component to further stabilize the feature representations is also a well-motivated contribution.

*   **Significance:**  Sparse-view CT reconstruction is a crucial area in medical imaging due to its potential to reduce radiation exposure and scan time.  Improving the quality of reconstructions from limited data has a direct clinical impact. The results presented in the paper seem to demonstrate a tangible improvement over existing methods, which would be valuable for clinical application. The emphasis on preserving detail and anatomical structure, compared to methods that optimize for MSE which leads to smooth outputs, has a significance importance in medical imaging.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-articulated motivation for the proposed method.
    *   The method is technically sound, with a clear description of the architecture and training procedure.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with comparisons against multiple baselines, including both classical and state-of-the-art generative models.
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component (contrastive learning and autoregressive constraint).
    *   Qualitative results (Figures 5, 6, and 7) visually support the quantitative improvements claimed by the authors, which is essential in medical imaging.
    *   The code availability promotes reproducibility and further research in the area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the quantitative results are generally strong, some differences between CLS-DM and X2CT-CNN performance on the LIDC dataset, especially for lateral and 2-view, need more explanation. The authors do attempt to justify the reduced PSNR/SSIM through the nature of the loss.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the limitations of the proposed method. For example, how robust is the method to different types of anatomical structures, varying levels of noise in the X-ray images, or changes in the number/angles of the sparse views.

*   **Potential Impact:**  If validated with more extensive clinical data and tested for generalization across different scanners and patient populations, the CLS-DM method has the potential to improve the clinical utility of sparse-view CT.  The combination of contrastive learning and diffusion models could also inspire new approaches to other cross-modal medical imaging tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the field of sparse-view CT reconstruction.  The proposed CLS-DM method effectively addresses a key challenge (latent space alignment) through a technically sound and well-evaluated approach. While the paper could benefit from a deeper discussion of limitations, the results are compelling and the code availability is a major strength. The novelty, while not groundbreaking, is well-motivated and results in tangible improvements over existing methods.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixture of Experts in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11181v1)
- **Authors**: Danyang Zhang, Junhao Song, Ziqian Bi, Yingfang Yuan, Tianyang Wang, Joe Yeong, Junfeng Hao
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a comprehensive review of the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture in large language models, highlighting its ability to significantly enhance model performance while maintaining minimal computational overhead. Through a systematic analysis spanning theoretical foundations, core architectural designs, and large language model (LLM) applications, we examine expert gating and routing mechanisms, hierarchical and sparse MoE configurations, meta-learning approaches, multimodal and multitask learning scenarios, real-world deployment cases, and recent advances and challenges in deep learning. Our analysis identifies key advantages of MoE, including superior model capacity compared to equivalent Bayesian approaches, improved task-specific performance, and the ability to scale model capacity efficiently. We also underscore the importance of ensuring expert diversity, accurate calibration, and reliable inference aggregation, as these are essential for maximizing the effectiveness of MoE architectures. Finally, this review outlines current research limitations, open challenges, and promising future directions, providing a foundation for continued innovation in MoE architecture and its applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive review of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures within large language models (LLMs). It systematically examines various aspects of MoE, including theoretical foundations, architectural designs (expert gating, routing mechanisms, hierarchical and sparse configurations), meta-learning integration, multimodal and multitask learning applications, real-world deployments, and current challenges. The review highlights the advantages of MoEs, such as superior model capacity, improved task-specific performance, and efficient scaling, while also underscoring the importance of expert diversity, calibration, and inference aggregation. It concludes by outlining research limitations, open challenges, and future research directions. The paper provides a taxonomy of MoE models across different domains, a comparison of routing strategies, and detailed diagrams illustrating architectural variations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper, as a survey, does not introduce entirely *new* methods or architectures. However, its **novelty lies in its comprehensive synthesis and organization of existing knowledge**. It compiles and categorizes a vast amount of research in a rapidly evolving field. It identifies key trends, challenges, and future directions, which is valuable for researchers and practitioners alike. The detailed classification of MoE models, routing techniques, and architectural innovations is well-structured and provides a clear overview of the landscape. The connection to meta-learning and real-world applications are particularly helpful in understanding the broader impact.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is **high, especially given the increasing importance of MoE architectures in scaling LLMs**. MoEs are becoming a critical solution for creating more powerful and efficient models, and a comprehensive survey like this helps to consolidate understanding and guide future research. The paper addresses key questions regarding the practical deployment of MoEs (scalability, stability, expert diversity) and the limitations of existing approaches. Highlighting those limitations and future direction are critical to the continued development of the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey covers a broad range of topics related to MoEs, from theoretical underpinnings to practical deployment considerations.
    *   **Clear Organization:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow, with clear headings, diagrams, and tables.
    *   **Practical Insights:** It highlights the trade-offs involved in MoE design, such as the balance between model accuracy, application performance, and deployment cost.
    *   **Future Directions:** The concluding section provides a valuable roadmap for future research, identifying open challenges and promising areas for exploration.
    *   **Extensive References**: The paper offers a vast resource of relevant research for the reader

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Critical Depth:** While the paper offers a comprehensive overview, it could benefit from a more critical and comparative analysis of the different approaches. Deeper insights into the strengths and weaknesses of competing methods would enhance its value.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Analysis:** While it mentions performance metrics, it doesn't delve deeply into the quantitative comparisons of different MoE approaches across a standardized set of benchmarks.
    *   **Static Nature:** Given the rapid pace of development in this field, a survey can quickly become outdated. The paper's value will depend on its ability to remain relevant and updated over time.

*   **Potential Influence:** This survey has the potential to be widely cited and influential in the field. It provides a valuable resource for researchers seeking to understand the current state-of-the-art in MoE architectures and identify promising directions for future research. It should prove to be a fundamental resource for newcomers to the field as well.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper. While it doesn't present novel algorithms or techniques, its value lies in its comprehensive synthesis, organization, and critical overview of a rapidly evolving and important field. It significantly contributes by consolidating knowledge, identifying key challenges, and highlighting promising future directions. While it could be improved by adding more quantitative analysis and critical comparisons, its overall quality and potential influence warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Score**: 8/10

### Temperature and Persona Shape LLM Agent Consensus With Minimal Accuracy Gains in Qualitative Coding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11198v1)
- **Authors**: Conrad Borchers, Bahar Shahrokhian, Francesco Balzan, Elham Tajik, Sreecharan Sankaranarayanan, Sebastian Simon
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) enable new possibilities for qualitative research at scale, including coding and data annotation. While multi-agent systems (MAS) can emulate human coding workflows, their benefits over single-agent coding remain poorly understood. We conducted an experimental study of how agent persona and temperature shape consensus-building and coding accuracy of dialog segments based on a codebook with 8 codes. Our open-source MAS mirrors deductive human coding through structured agent discussion and consensus arbitration. Using six open-source LLMs (with 3 to 32 billion parameters) and 18 experimental configurations, we analyze over 77,000 coding decisions against a gold-standard dataset of human-annotated transcripts from online math tutoring sessions. Temperature significantly impacted whether and when consensus was reached across all six LLMs. MAS with multiple personas (including neutral, assertive, or empathetic), significantly delayed consensus in four out of six LLMs compared to uniform personas. In three of those LLMs, higher temperatures significantly diminished the effects of multiple personas on consensus. However, neither temperature nor persona pairing lead to robust improvements in coding accuracy. Single agents matched or outperformed MAS consensus in most conditions. Only one model (OpenHermesV2:7B) and code category showed above-chance gains from MAS deliberation when temperature was 0.5 or lower and especially when the agents included at least one assertive persona. Qualitative analysis of MAS collaboration for these configurations suggests that MAS may nonetheless aid in narrowing ambiguous code applications that could improve codebooks and human-AI coding. We contribute new insight into the limits of LLM-based qualitative methods, challenging the notion that diverse MAS personas lead to better outcomes. We open-source our MAS and experimentation code.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how temperature (randomness) and persona (simulated personality) impact the performance of multi-agent systems (MAS) for qualitative coding using large language models (LLMs). The authors conduct an experimental study using six open-source LLMs, varying temperature and persona pairings, to code dialog segments based on a predefined codebook. They evaluate consensus-building and coding accuracy against a human-annotated gold standard. The key findings are that temperature significantly affects consensus, persona pairing has a model-specific impact on consensus, but neither consistently improves coding accuracy. In fact, single-agent coding often matches or outperforms MAS consensus. Qualitative analysis reveals limitations in the reasoning and consistency of LLM agents, suggesting MAS may be more valuable for codebook refinement than automated coding itself. The authors release their MAS and experimentation code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Experimentation:** The paper conducts a well-designed, factorial experiment to evaluate the impact of temperature and persona on MAS for qualitative coding. This provides a rigorous foundation for their findings.
    *   **Open Source LLMs:** Using open-source LLMs makes the research more reproducible and accessible compared to relying solely on proprietary models like GPT-4.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper includes both quantitative analysis of coding accuracy and qualitative analysis of agent deliberation traces, providing a multi-faceted perspective on MAS performance.
    *   **Challenging Assumptions:** The study directly challenges the often-assumed benefits of MAS for qualitative coding, demonstrating that simply adding agents and varying personalities does not automatically improve accuracy.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers valuable insights into the limitations of current LLM-based MAS for qualitative research, highlighting the need for human oversight and potential applications in codebook refinement.
    * **Code Release:** The release of the code allows for reproduction and extension of the work, increasing its value to the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Context:** The study is conducted within a specific context (online math tutoring transcripts) using a pre-defined codebook. While this provides a controlled environment, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other domains or more exploratory coding tasks.
    *   **Narrow Persona Definitions:** The persona definitions, while systematic, are relatively simple (neutral, assertive, empathetic). More nuanced or complex personas might yield different results.
    *   **Modest Performance Improvements:** While the paper identifies a specific scenario where MAS improves coding (OpenHermesV2:7B and Guiding Feedback), the overall accuracy gains are small. The absence of large improvements limits the practical appeal of the proposed approach.
    *   **Focus on Deductive Coding:** The study focuses on deductive coding. The value of MAS may be higher in inductive coding where exploration and discovering new codes is a goal.
    * **Limited LLMs:** Testing with different LLMs architectures would increase the paper's generalizability.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The systematic exploration of temperature and persona effects in MAS for qualitative coding is relatively novel. Prior work has often focused on architectural design or high-level comparisons without rigorous parameter tuning.
    *   The finding that MAS does not reliably improve coding accuracy, and can even decrease it, is significant. This contradicts the common narrative and prompts a re-evaluation of MAS utility in qualitative research.
    *   The identification of potential applications in codebook refinement is a valuable contribution, shifting the focus from fully automated coding to a more collaborative human-AI approach.
    *   The qualitative insights into the reasoning and consistency of LLM agents offer valuable guidance for future MAS design and the development of more reliable and interpretable qualitative coding systems.

    Despite some limitations, the paper provides a valuable contribution by rigorously evaluating the impact of temperature and personality in MAS for qualitative coding. Its findings challenge prevailing assumptions and suggest a more nuanced perspective on the role of LLMs in qualitative research. The combination of quantitative analysis and qualitative insights, along with the public release of code and data, enhances the paper's significance.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper's strengths lie in its systematic methodology, comprehensive analysis, and challenging of assumptions. The finding that MAS does not reliably improve accuracy is particularly significant, pushing the field to re-evaluate current approaches. However, the limited context, narrow persona definitions, and small performance gains slightly temper the overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EsBBQ and CaBBQ: The Spanish and Catalan Bias Benchmarks for Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11216v1)
- **Authors**: Valle Ruiz-Fernández, Mario Mina, Júlia Falcão, Luis Vasquez-Reina, Anna Sallés, Aitor Gonzalez-Agirre, Olatz Perez-de-Viñaspre
- **Abstract**: Previous literature has largely shown that Large Language Models (LLMs) perpetuate social biases learnt from their pre-training data. Given the notable lack of resources for social bias evaluation in languages other than English, and for social contexts outside of the United States, this paper introduces the Spanish and the Catalan Bias Benchmarks for Question Answering (EsBBQ and CaBBQ). Based on the original BBQ, these two parallel datasets are designed to assess social bias across 10 categories using a multiple-choice QA setting, now adapted to the Spanish and Catalan languages and to the social context of Spain. We report evaluation results on different LLMs, factoring in model family, size and variant. Our results show that models tend to fail to choose the correct answer in ambiguous scenarios, and that high QA accuracy often correlates with greater reliance on social biases.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces ESBBQ and CABBQ, two novel benchmarks designed to evaluate social biases in Large Language Models (LLMs) specifically within the Spanish and Catalan languages and cultural contexts. These benchmarks are adaptations and extensions of the original Bias Benchmark for Question Answering (BBQ), incorporating manually-adapted templates, culturally-relevant scenarios, and new templates derived from a public survey on prevalent stereotypes in Spain. The authors evaluate a range of LLMs on these benchmarks, analyzing their performance and reliance on social biases across ten categories (Age, Disability Status, Gender, LGBTQIA+, Nationality, Physical Appearance, Race/Ethnicity, Religion, Socioeconomic Status, and Spanish Region). The results indicate that models tend to struggle in ambiguous scenarios and that high QA accuracy often correlates with increased reliance on social biases. The datasets, templates, and code are released publicly under an open license.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in creating social bias benchmarks specifically tailored for Spanish and Catalan. Existing resources in this area have been skewed towards English and the US cultural context, making this a valuable and necessary contribution. The manual adaptation process, driven by a public survey and cultural expertise, adds significant value beyond simple translation, creating a more authentic and reliable evaluation tool.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in:
    *   Providing a much-needed resource for evaluating LLMs in languages other than English. This expands the scope of social bias research and helps ensure that LLMs are developed responsibly in a global context.
