    *   Highlighting the cultural specificity of social biases. The adaptation process demonstrates that biases are not universal and that evaluation benchmarks must be tailored to specific cultural contexts.
    *   Providing insights into the relationship between model performance and social bias. The finding that high QA accuracy can correlate with increased reliance on social biases is important for guiding future research and development.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Methodologically sound adaptation process involving public surveys and expert manual adaptation.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of several LLMs, considering model size, family, and variant.
    *   Public release of datasets, templates, and code, enabling further research and development.
    *   Clear articulation of the limitations of the study.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The survey responses were skewed towards majority groups despite efforts to encourage diverse participation, which might affect the representativeness of the stereotypes captured.
    *   The focus solely on stereotyping, while a valuable dimension of social bias, does not encompass all forms of social inequality.
    *   The datasets lack intersectional categories, treating identities as mutually exclusive.
    *   The Spanish-centric nature of the data limits its direct applicability outside the Spanish cultural context.
*   **Potential Influence:** The ESBBQ and CABBQ benchmarks have the potential to become valuable resources for researchers and practitioners working on LLMs in Spanish and Catalan. They can help to identify and mitigate social biases in these models, leading to more fair and inclusive AI systems. The adaptation process described in the paper can also serve as a template for creating similar benchmarks for other languages and cultures.

Given the novelty and significance of the contribution, alongside a rigorous and comprehensive methodology, but acknowledging the limitations stemming from the survey representativeness and the exclusion of intersectional categories, a score of 8 is warranted.

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### An Agentic Flow for Finite State Machine Extraction using Prompt Chaining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11222v1)
- **Authors**: Fares Wael, Youssef Maklad, Ali Hamdi, Wael Elsersy
- **Abstract**: Finite-State Machines (FSMs) are critical for modeling the operational logic of network protocols, enabling verification, analysis, and vulnerability discovery. However, existing FSM extraction techniques face limitations such as scalability, incomplete coverage, and ambiguity in natural language specifications. In this paper, we propose FlowFSM, a novel agentic framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) combined with prompt chaining and chain-of-thought reasoning to extract accurate FSMs from raw RFC documents. FlowFSM systematically processes protocol specifications, identifies state transitions, and constructs structured rule-books by chaining agent outputs. Experimental evaluation across FTP and RTSP protocols demonstrates that FlowFSM achieves high extraction precision while minimizing hallucinated transitions, showing promising results. Our findings highlight the potential of agent-based LLM systems in the advancement of protocol analysis and FSM inference for cybersecurity and reverse engineering applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FlowFSM, a novel framework for extracting Finite State Machines (FSMs) from raw RFC documents using Large Language Models (LLMs). FlowFSM employs a chain-of-thought reasoning approach combined with a structured prompt chaining strategy to systematically process protocol specifications, identify state transitions, and construct a rulebook representing the FSM. The approach decomposes the complex FSM extraction task into modular, interpretable steps, using each prompt output to build upon the previous one. The framework is evaluated on FTP and RTSP protocols, demonstrating improved extraction precision compared to potential hallucinated transitions. The authors highlight FlowFSM's potential for cybersecurity and reverse engineering applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to FSM extraction by combining prompt chaining and chain-of-thought reasoning using LLMs. While other works have explored FSM extraction with LLMs, FlowFSM provides a more structured agentic approach, decomposing the task into interpretable, modular steps. The use of CrewAI for agent orchestration is relatively unique in this specific application. The core novelty lies in the structured approach to LLM prompting, which aids in extracting FSMs with higher fidelity.
*   **Significance:** FSM extraction is a critical component in many areas of network security, including vulnerability analysis and protocol fuzzing. The accuracy of these models is paramount, and the method shows improved extraction precision, which reduces the likelihood of false positives. By reducing reliance on manual labor for model creation, this work also has the potential to improve the speed and scalability of protocol analysis. The experimental results across FTP and RTSP support the claims of effectiveness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The chain-of-thought and prompt chaining techniques break down the complexity of FSM extraction, leading to more reliable and interpretable results.
    *   The system provides a modular, agent-based architecture. This approach makes the overall framework extensible and adaptable to different protocol specifications and LLM models.
    *   The evaluation demonstrates solid results, indicating improved precision and balanced F1-scores compared to hallucinated transitions, supporting the efficacy of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper is limited in its protocol testing to only FTP and RTSP. Although this demonstrates its functionality, further testing is required to determine general applicability across a wider set of protocols.
    *   The paper hints at high computational and runtime costs. Without a detailed analysis of these costs, it is difficult to assess the practicality of the approach, especially considering large-scale network protocols.
    *   Although the CrewAI framework is used, there is limited discussion on the specifics of the multi-agent collaboration strategy, how the agent roles are defined, and how they interact to coordinate the overall FSM extraction process.
    *   No comparison is provided with other methods, so there's nothing to contextualize the benefit of this method other than "reduced hallucinations".

*   **Potential Influence:** The work contributes to the growing body of literature on using LLMs for cybersecurity tasks. FlowFSM presents a practical methodology for protocol analysis and reverse engineering, paving the way for more automated and scalable vulnerability assessment and security monitoring.

**Justification for Score:**

FlowFSM presents a novel and promising methodology for FSM extraction using prompt chaining and chain-of-thought reasoning within an agent-based framework. The structured approach represents an advancement compared to traditional LLM-based FSM extraction techniques. The experimental results support the paper's claims, demonstrating good extraction performance across FTP and RTSP protocols. However, the limited scope of the evaluation, lack of performance analysis (computational costs), and no comparison with other methods temper the overall impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sparse Autoencoders Can Capture Language-Specific Concepts Across Diverse Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11230v1)
- **Authors**: Lyzander Marciano Andrylie, Inaya Rahmanisa, Mahardika Krisna Ihsani, Alfan Farizki Wicaksono, Haryo Akbarianto Wibowo, Alham Fikri Aji
- **Abstract**: Understanding the multilingual mechanisms of large language models (LLMs) provides insight into how they process different languages, yet this remains challenging. Existing studies often focus on individual neurons, but their polysemantic nature makes it difficult to isolate language-specific units from cross-lingual representations. To address this, we explore sparse autoencoders (SAEs) for their ability to learn monosemantic features that represent concrete and abstract concepts across languages in LLMs. While some of these features are language-independent, the presence of language-specific features remains underexplored. In this work, we introduce SAE-LAPE, a method based on feature activation probability, to identify language-specific features within the feed-forward network. We find that many such features predominantly appear in the middle to final layers of the model and are interpretable. These features influence the model's multilingual performance and language output and can be used for language identification with performance comparable to fastText along with more interpretability. Our code is available at https://github.com/LyzanderAndrylie/language-specific-features .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Sparse Autoencoders Can Capture Language-Specific Concepts Across Diverse Languages":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenge of understanding multilingual mechanisms in large language models (LLMs), focusing on how they process different languages.  Instead of the common approach of analyzing individual neurons (which are often polysemantic), the authors explore sparse autoencoders (SAEs) for their ability to learn monosemantic features that represent concrete and abstract concepts. Their key contribution is introducing SAE-LAPE, a method based on feature activation probability, designed to identify language-specific features within the feed-forward network (FFN) of LLMs.  They find that many language-specific features appear in the middle to final layers of the model and are interpretable. They demonstrate that these features influence multilingual performance, language output (steering the LLM), and can be used for language identification, achieving performance comparable to fastText.  The code is made available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using SAEs to find language-specific features in LLMs, while not entirely novel (sparse autoencoders have been used for this), is a reasonably clever approach. Applying it to a multilingual context and explicitly trying to isolate language-specific *vs.* cross-lingual representations is useful. SAE-LAPE, as a modification of Tang et al.'s method that's tailored for SAEs, is the core of their novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in attempting to bridge the gap between high-level multilingual performance and the underlying linguistic representations learned by LLMs. Identifying and isolating language-specific features could lead to better control and understanding of multilingual generation, translation, and other tasks. The language steering experiments, while initial, provide some proof of concept regarding the utility of these features. The language identification task also reinforces the finding that these features encode meaningful language information.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of understanding multilingual mechanisms in LLMs.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:**  The SAE-LAPE method is clearly explained, and the modifications from Tang et al. are justified. The experimental setup is also described in detail.
    *   **Empirical validation:**  The paper includes experiments that demonstrate the existence, interpretability, and influence of language-specific features.
    *   **Code availability:** Making the code available enhances reproducibility and encourages further research.
    *   **Interpretability analysis:** The use of automated interpretability tools to analyze the identified features is a strong point, providing a more systematic and objective analysis than relying solely on manual inspection.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model and Dataset:** The study is limited to Llama 3.2 1B, a relatively smaller model, and the findings might not generalize well to larger, more advanced models. The dataset used for analysis is also relatively small.
    *   **Steering Results:** The steering results are somewhat mixed and require further investigation.  The increased incoherence in text generation when manipulating specific features is a significant challenge.
    *   **SAEs Classifier limitations:** There are some limitations on the reliance on SAEs classifier such as the potential bias of towards languages with more features, especially if multiple languages appear in an input. There is little validation for some features used in specific tasks.
    *   **Dependence on existing SAEs.** Using pretrained SAEs may limit the capability of discovering novelties tailored to specific LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in:

    *   **Multilingual LLM understanding:** It offers a method for analyzing the internal representations of multilingual models beyond individual neurons.
    *   **Controllable generation:** The language steering experiments suggest potential for controlling language output by manipulating specific features.
    *   **Cross-lingual transfer learning:** Further investigation of cross-lingual and language-specific features could provide insights into how to improve transfer learning in LLMs.

* Score: 6

*   **Justification:**

I'm assigning a score of 6 because while the approach is interesting and shows some promising results, the paper has some considerable limitations and doesn't revolutionize the field. The findings are somewhat specific to Llama 3.2 1B and the particular SAEs used. The steering experiments need more robust analysis, and there's room for further improvement in the proposed method to be able to scale towards a new application. If future work successfully address the limited model size, the mixed results in steering, and the reliance on available resources, it is conceivable that the influence of the current paper would grow. For now, it is reasonably novel and makes a solid, incremental contribution to the understanding of multilingual LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### MFGDiffusion: Mask-Guided Smoke Synthesis for Enhanced Forest Fire Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11252v1)
- **Authors**: Guanghao Wu, Chen Xu, Hai Song, Chong Wang, Qixing Zhang
- **Abstract**: Smoke is the first visible indicator of a wildfire.With the advancement of deep learning, image-based smoke detection has become a crucial method for detecting and preventing forest fires. However, the scarcity of smoke image data from forest fires is one of the significant factors hindering the detection of forest fire smoke. Image generation models offer a promising solution for synthesizing realistic smoke images. However, current inpainting models exhibit limitations in generating high-quality smoke representations, particularly manifesting as inconsistencies between synthesized smoke and background contexts. To solve these problems, we proposed a comprehensive framework for generating forest fire smoke images. Firstly, we employed the pre-trained segmentation model and the multimodal model to obtain smoke masks and image captions.Then, to address the insufficient utilization of masks and masked images by inpainting models, we introduced a network architecture guided by mask and masked image features. We also proposed a new loss function, the mask random difference loss, which enhances the consistency of the generated effects around the mask by randomly expanding and eroding the mask edges.Finally, to generate a smoke image dataset using random masks for subsequent detection tasks, we incorporated smoke characteristics and use a multimodal large language model as a filtering tool to select diverse and reasonable smoke images, thereby improving the quality of the synthetic dataset. Experiments showed that our generated smoke images are realistic and diverse, and effectively enhance the performance of forest fire smoke detection models. Code is available at https://github.com/wghr123/MFGDiffusion.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MFGDiffusion, a mask-guided diffusion-based method for synthesizing realistic smoke images for enhanced forest fire detection.  The approach addresses the limitations of existing image inpainting models in generating high-quality smoke representations, particularly concerning consistency between synthesized smoke and background.  The method involves: 1) An automated pipeline to generate smoke masks and image captions using SAM and Blip2, 2) A novel network architecture, MFGDiffusion, that utilizes mask and masked image features through a joint cross-attention mechanism and a mask random difference loss to enhance contextual consistency.  3) A data filtering mechanism incorporating smoke characteristics and a multimodal large language model to select diverse and reasonable smoke images.  Experiments demonstrate the realism and diversity of the generated smoke images and their effectiveness in improving forest fire smoke detection model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper proposes a complete pipeline, from data acquisition and synthesis to filtering and application, making it a practical solution for generating smoke datasets.
    *   **Novel Network Architecture:** The MFGDiffusion architecture, with its joint cross-attention mechanism and mask random difference loss, offers a clever solution to the limitations of standard inpainting models for smoke synthesis. The random mask difference loss is a genuinely original contribution.
    *   **Multimodal Integration:**  The use of SAM, Blip2, and large language models for captioning, mask generation, and data filtering showcases a good understanding of leveraging current state-of-the-art models. The multimodal filtering based on tailored smoke properties is valuable.
    *   **Improved Performance:** The experiments convincingly demonstrate that the synthesized data improves the performance of smoke detection models compared to using only real data or data augmented with other methods. The quantitative and qualitative results support the claims well.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Making the code publicly available is a major strength that promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the components are well-integrated and the mask random difference loss is original, the overall approach relies heavily on existing pre-trained models. The core novelty is in the specific architecture for smoke generation, not in entirely new deep learning concepts.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation focuses primarily on forest fire smoke detection.  It would be beneficial to explore the generalizability of the generated images in other applications involving smoke analysis.
    *   **Subjectivity in Data Filtering:** Although scoring principles are established for data filtering, the manual scoring involves subjective factors and can potentially introduce bias.

*   **Novelty:** The mask random difference loss and the integration of multimodal tools for data filtering constitute the most novel contributions. The architectural changes, while effective, are built on well-established techniques.

*   **Significance:** Generating high-quality, diverse smoke datasets is crucial for improving forest fire detection.  This paper makes a significant contribution by providing a practical and effective method for achieving this.  The availability of the code will enable other researchers to easily use and extend the method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The approach is likely to be adopted by researchers working on smoke detection and other related fields. The method could also be adapted for generating synthetic data for other types of atmospheric phenomena.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed approach to synthetic smoke data generation. The combination of various existing tools with a novel loss function and architecture, specifically tailored for smoke synthesis, leads to significant improvements in downstream tasks. However, the lack of groundbreaking novelty in terms of core deep learning concepts and some subjectivity inherent in the data filtering slightly lowers the score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Empirical Study of Multi-Agent RAG for Real-World University Admissions Counseling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11272v1)
- **Authors**: Anh Nguyen-Duc, Chien Vu Manh, Bao Anh Tran, Viet Phuong Ngo, Luan Le Chi, Anh Quang Nguyen
- **Abstract**: This paper presents MARAUS (Multi-Agent and Retrieval-Augmented University Admission System), a real-world deployment of a conversational AI platform for higher education admissions counseling in Vietnam. While large language models (LLMs) offer potential for automating advisory tasks, most existing solutions remain limited to prototypes or synthetic benchmarks. MARAUS addresses this gap by combining hybrid retrieval, multi-agent orchestration, and LLM-based generation into a system tailored for real-world university admissions. In collaboration with the University of Transport Technology (UTT) in Hanoi, we conducted a two-phase study involving technical development and real-world evaluation. MARAUS processed over 6,000 actual user interactions, spanning six categories of queries. Results show substantial improvements over LLM-only baselines: on average 92 percent accuracy, hallucination rates reduced from 15 precent to 1.45 percent, and average response times below 4 seconds. The system operated cost-effectively, with a two-week deployment cost of 11.58 USD using GPT-4o mini. This work provides actionable insights for the deployment of agentic RAG systems in low-resource educational settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MARAUS, a multi-agent retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system designed for real-world university admissions counseling in Vietnam. MARAUS combines hybrid retrieval, multi-agent orchestration, and LLM-based generation to address complex and nuanced queries from prospective students and their parents.  The system was deployed at the University of Transport Technology (UTT) in Hanoi. The paper presents empirical results showing that MARAUS significantly outperforms LLM-only baselines in terms of accuracy, hallucination reduction, and response time. The paper highlights the cost-effectiveness of the system, built on GPT-4.0 mini. It argues that MARAUS provides actionable insights for deploying agentic RAG systems in low-resource educational settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its *real-world deployment and evaluation* of a multi-agent RAG system for a specific domain (university admissions) in a low-resource language (Vietnamese). While the individual components of the system (hybrid retrieval, multi-agent orchestration, LLMs) are not entirely novel, their *integration and tuning for this specific application* demonstrate practical engineering value. The focus on reducing hallucinations in a resource-constrained setting is another positive aspect. However, it is not extremely novel in terms of algorithm design.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its practical contributions. It demonstrates a feasible and effective approach to automating complex advisory tasks in a context where human resources are limited. The empirical results, derived from over 6,000 real user interactions, provide convincing evidence of MARAUS's performance. The focus on cost-effectiveness, especially with the use of GPT-4.0 mini, is important for real-world applicability. The insights on adapting RAG for a low-resource language are valuable to the wider NLP community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Real-world deployment:** This is a major strength. Moving beyond synthetic benchmarks to a real operational environment is crucial for demonstrating the practical utility of RAG systems.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The extensive empirical evaluation with a significant volume of user interactions provides strong support for the claims.
    *   **Focus on hallucination reduction:** A critical issue for real-world applications, especially in high-stakes domains like education.
    *   **Cost-effectiveness analysis:** This is crucial for adoption in resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Clear system architecture and implementation details:** The paper provides sufficient information to allow others to replicate or adapt the system.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The case study is specific to UTT and the Vietnamese admissions context. While the authors argue for broader applicability to similar institutions, the results might not directly translate to other domains or cultural settings.
    *   **Limited Comparisons:** While comparisons are made to an LLM-only baseline and related RAG implementations, a more extensive comparison to other state-of-the-art RAG techniques would have strengthened the paper.
    *   **Limited Depth of Qualitative Analysis:** While user satisfaction is measured, a more in-depth qualitative analysis of user interactions and the impact of MARAUS on the admissions process would provide richer insights.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by showcasing a real-world application of a multi-agent RAG system in a low-resource environment. While the individual components of the system aren't groundbreaking in isolation, their integration and adaptation to the specific domain provide valuable engineering insights. The empirical results provide strong evidence of the system's effectiveness and cost-efficiency. The primary limitations are the case-study nature of the work and the limited scope of comparative analysis.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's strong practical contribution and real-world validation (significant strengths) but acknowledges the limitations related to generalizability and comparative analysis. A score of 7.5 signifies that the paper presents a valuable contribution with solid evidence, but stops short of being a highly transformative breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### KV-Latent: Dimensional-level KV Cache Reduction with Frequency-aware Rotary Positional Embedding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11273v1)
- **Authors**: Luohe Shi, Zuchao Li, Lefei Zhang, Guoming Liu, Baoyuan Qi, Hai Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) based on Transformer Decoders have become the preferred choice for conversational generative AI. Despite the overall superiority of the Decoder architecture, the gradually increasing Key-Value (KV) cache during inference has emerged as a primary efficiency bottleneck, both in aspects of memory consumption and data transfer bandwidth limitations. To address these challenges, we propose a paradigm called KV-Latent. By down-sampling the Key-Value vector dimensions into a latent space, we can significantly reduce the KV Cache footprint and improve inference speed, only with a small amount of extra training, less than 1\% of pre-training takes. Besides, we enhanced the stability of Rotary Positional Embedding applied on lower-dimensional vectors by modifying its frequency sampling mechanism, avoiding noise introduced by higher frequencies while retaining position attenuation. Our experiments, including both models with Grouped Query Attention and those without, have yielded satisfactory results. Finally, we conducted comparative experiments to study the impact of separately reducing Key and Value components on model's performance. Our approach allows for the construction of more efficient language model systems, and opens the new possibility on KV Cache saving and efficient LLMs. Our code is available at https://github.com/ShiLuohe/KV-Latent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KV-Latent, a novel approach to reduce the Key-Value (KV) cache size in large language models (LLMs) based on Transformer decoders. It achieves this by down-sampling the Key and Value vector dimensions into a latent space and then reconstructing them using Query-transpose and Output transformations. The method requires only a small amount of additional training. The authors also address the stability issues of Rotary Positional Embedding (RoPE) at lower dimensions by modifying its frequency sampling mechanism.  Experiments on models with and without Grouped Query Attention (GQA) show promising results.  The paper analyzes the impact of separately reducing Key and Value components, suggesting that Value reduction has a greater impact on performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of dimensional reduction for KV caches *and* the frequency-aware RoPE modification. Down-sampling KV caches *per se* is not entirely new, as MQA and GQA implicitly achieve this, and SVD-based methods have been explored. However, the explicit dimensional reduction at the *head level*, coupled with the 2-stage training strategy, and the analysis of Key vs. Value dimension reduction provides some incremental novelty. The frequency-aware RoPE modification is a more significant contribution, especially for lower dimensional representations, as it addresses a concrete instability issue. Decoupling the constraint *dh* * *nh* = *h*, allowing explicit control over head size, is a meaningful contribution.

*   **Significance:**  The significance is moderate. Reducing KV cache size is a crucial problem for LLM deployment, and any method that improves efficiency without significantly sacrificing performance is valuable. The demonstrated reduction in KV cache size with minimal performance loss makes this a potentially useful technique for real-world applications. The analysis of Key and Value head size impact offers insight into the model's attention mechanism. The frequency-aware RoPE is valuable as it improves the robustness of models in low-dimensional embedding scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained methodology, including the two-stage training and frequency-aware RoPE modification.
    *   Empirical evaluation with multiple benchmarks and model architectures.
    *   Analysis of Key vs. Value dimension reduction.
    *   Addresses a specific issue with RoPE at lower dimensions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited novelty regarding KV cache reduction itself; builds upon existing techniques.
    *   The experiments involve only a limited amount of fine-tuning data.  The performance recovery might not generalize to different datasets or larger training regimes.
    *   The performance gains from the frequency-aware RoPE modification are not extensively quantified; it's primarily demonstrated as a stability improvement.
    *   The comparison with other methods (especially methods like CLA) is acknowledged as not directly comparable, which somewhat weakens the evaluation.
    *   The potential for security vulnerabilities introduced through KV cache compression/modification are not explored. The paper is focused on efficiency.

*   **Impact:** The paper's impact is likely to be moderate. The KV-Latent paradigm provides a practical approach to reduce KV cache size, which is relevant to many researchers and practitioners working on LLM deployment. The findings regarding Key/Value dimensions and RoPE stability could inform future model design. However, it is unlikely to be a revolutionary breakthrough, but rather a useful incremental improvement. The code availability is a plus.

**Justification of Score:**

The score reflects the paper's incremental novelty and moderate significance. While the KV cache reduction aspect builds upon existing techniques, the combination with the frequency-aware RoPE and the head-level dimension analysis adds value. The experimental results are promising, but the limited fine-tuning data and lack of direct comparison with certain other methods limit the strength of the conclusions. The paper provides practical solutions and useful insights, justifying the assigned score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### FMC: Formalization of Natural Language Mathematical Competition Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11275v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxuan Xie, Chengwu Liu, Ye Yuan, Siqi Li, Zhiping Xiao, Ming Zhang
- **Abstract**: Efficient and accurate autoformalization methods, which leverage large-scale datasets of extensive natural language mathematical problems to construct formal language datasets, are key to advancing formal mathematical reasoning. In this paper, we propose an autoformalization pipeline based on large language models with error feedback, achieving a fully automatic and training-free formalization approach. Using this pipeline, we curate an Olympiad-level dataset aligning natural language problems with Lean formalizations. The dataset comprises $3,922$ mathematical problems in natural language and $9,787$ in Lean, of which $64.46\%$ were assessed as at least above-average quality, making it suitable as a benchmark for automated theorem provers. Additionally, we investigate the formalization and reasoning capabilities of various LLMs and empirically demonstrate that few-shot learning, error feedback, and increasing sampling numbers enhance the autoformalization process. Experiments of three automated theorem provers on the \dataset\ dataset also highlight its challenging nature and its value as a benchmark for formal reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "FMC," an autoformalization pipeline designed to convert natural language mathematical competition problems into Lean formalizations. This pipeline uses large language models (LLMs) and incorporates error feedback to improve translation accuracy. The pipeline stages include formal translation, formal verification, back translation, and consistency checks. The authors curate a dataset of Olympiad-level problems aligned with Lean formalizations using this pipeline, comprising 3,922 natural language problems and 9,787 Lean statements. The paper evaluates the pipeline's performance using metrics like syntactic validity and semantic consistency, as well as a quality assessment using a large language model, and benchmarks automated theorem provers on the resulting dataset. The paper demonstrates the efficacy of error feedback and increased sampling in enhancing the autoformalization process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for autoformalization is not entirely new, as the paper acknowledges the existence of prior work like the Lean Workbook. However, the key novelty lies in the enhanced pipeline incorporating error feedback, which distinguishes it from existing approaches and addresses the challenge of limited formal data. The rigorous quality check using a LLM also demonstrates novelty.
* **Significance:** Creating large, high-quality datasets of formal mathematical statements is crucial for advancing formal mathematical reasoning and automated theorem proving. The FMC dataset, focused on Olympiad-level problems, fills a specific gap in the available resources. The dataset's difficulty provides a valuable benchmark for testing and improving automated theorem provers. Moreover, the error feedback mechanism in the autoformalization pipeline is a significant step toward creating more robust and accurate formalization tools.
* **Strengths:**
    * The error feedback mechanism is a significant improvement over previous autoformalization pipelines.
    * The focus on Olympiad-level problems ensures a certain level of difficulty and quality.
    * The dataset quality assessment provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of the formalizations.
    * The benchmarking of automated theorem provers validates the utility of the dataset.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The exclusion of geometry problems is a significant limitation, as geometry is a core area of mathematics and mathematical competitions. The authors' reasoning that LLMs struggle with geometry is understandable, but this omission reduces the dataset's overall scope and utility.
    * The paper mentions that misjudgments still occur during consistency checks with even the best LLMs. More detailed analysis of the *types* of errors that persist despite the pipeline would be valuable.  What kinds of mathematical subtleties are LLMs still missing?
    *  While the pipeline is presented as "training-free," the authors use few-shot learning. The reliance on the quality and representativeness of the few-shot examples should be acknowledged as a potential limitation. The selection of these few-shot examples and their impact on the results could have been explored further.
    * Due to computational limitations, only five samples were taken per problem. This could be potentially increased for higher accuracy.

* **Impact:** The FMC dataset has the potential to significantly impact the development of automated theorem provers and formal verification tools. By providing a challenging benchmark, it can drive research in these areas. The error feedback mechanism could also be adopted and further refined in other autoformalization projects.  The insights gained about the limitations of LLMs in this domain will also guide future research directions. However, the exclusion of geometric content and existing misjudgements during consistency check might potentially hinder the research in some directions.
* **Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by presenting a novel autoformalization pipeline and curating a challenging and useful dataset. The error feedback mechanism is a particularly noteworthy innovation.  The dataset quality, confirmed by both automated analysis and quality assessment, indicates its value as a benchmark.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates solid technical merit with a novel approach to autoformalization and the creation of a valuable resource for the community. While the paper is not revolutionary, as autoformalization is an active field, the error feedback mechanism and the focus on Olympiad-level problems set it apart. However, the exclusion of geometry problems and persistent limitations of the consistency check model reduce the overall impact and potential scope of the work. A higher score would require overcoming the key limitations mentioned above and making stronger progress in the mathematical areas not yet addressed by the project.


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Taming Uncertainty via Automation: Observing, Analyzing, and Optimizing Agentic AI Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11277v1)
- **Authors**: Dany Moshkovich, Sergey Zeltyn
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed within agentic systems-collections of interacting, LLM-powered agents that execute complex, adaptive workflows using memory, tools, and dynamic planning. While enabling powerful new capabilities, these systems also introduce unique forms of uncertainty stemming from probabilistic reasoning, evolving memory states, and fluid execution paths. Traditional software observability and operations practices fall short in addressing these challenges. This paper introduces AgentOps: a comprehensive framework for observing, analyzing, optimizing, and automating operation of agentic AI systems. We identify distinct needs across four key roles-developers, testers, site reliability engineers (SREs), and business users-each of whom engages with the system at different points in its lifecycle. We present the AgentOps Automation Pipeline, a six-stage process encompassing behavior observation, metric collection, issue detection, root cause analysis, optimized recommendations, and runtime automation. Throughout, we emphasize the critical role of automation in managing uncertainty and enabling self-improving AI systems-not by eliminating uncertainty, but by taming it to ensure safe, adaptive, and effective operation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AgentOps, a comprehensive framework designed to address the challenges of managing and optimizing agentic AI systems, which are increasingly leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs). These systems, while powerful, introduce new forms of uncertainty due to probabilistic reasoning, evolving memory, and dynamic execution paths. AgentOps aims to observe, analyze, optimize, and automate the operation of these systems, catering to the needs of developers, testers, SREs, and business users. The paper proposes a six-stage automation pipeline encompassing behavior observation, metric collection, issue detection, root cause analysis, optimized recommendations, and runtime automation. A key emphasis is placed on the importance of automation in taming uncertainty, enabling self-improving AI systems while ensuring safe and effective operation. The paper outlines the AgentOps framework taxonomy and links its components to various user roles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of "AgentOps" as a distinct discipline is reasonably novel, particularly its focus on the unique challenges introduced by LLM-powered agentic systems. While elements of observability and AIOps exist, the paper attempts to tailor these concepts to the specific characteristics of AI agents (e.g., probabilistic decision-making, memory, dynamic execution). The six-stage pipeline offers a structured approach, which is a step forward, but many of the individual stages leverage existing techniques from broader areas like software engineering and machine learning. The specific application and integration of these techniques into a unified framework focused on agentic systems are where the novelty lies. The taxonomy provided is a useful organizational tool, but not necessarily groundbreaking in itself. The paper falls short of introducing a completely new analytical technique or fundamentally changing existing methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the growing prevalence and importance of agentic AI systems. As these systems are adopted more widely, the need for robust operational frameworks becomes critical. AgentOps, if adopted, could provide a valuable tool for managing complexity, ensuring reliability, and optimizing performance. Addressing the challenges associated with monitoring, debugging, and improving LLM-based agents is an important task, but the paper only outlines the framework. The paper doesn't provide empirical results or case studies demonstrating the practical effectiveness of the AgentOps framework which limits the initial significance. The discussion on taming uncertainty is well-articulated, emphasizing the importance of managing rather than eliminating uncertainty in intelligent systems, which is a practical perspective. A weakness is the lack of comparison to existing frameworks or tools within AIOps (aside from mentioning some observability platforms). A thorough evaluation of AgentOps against alternative approaches is missing. The paper also doesn't address the complexities involved in implementing such a framework in real-world scenarios, considering factors like data privacy, security, and resource constraints.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Identifies a critical problem space related to the management and operation of agentic AI systems.
    *   Presents a structured framework (AgentOps) that addresses key challenges across the system lifecycle.
    *   Recognizes the distinct needs of different user roles (developers, testers, SREs, business users).
    *   Emphasizes the importance of automation for managing uncertainty and enabling self-improving systems.
    *   Provides a useful taxonomy for understanding the components of an AgentOps framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Lacks empirical validation or case studies to demonstrate the practical effectiveness of the AgentOps framework.
    *   The framework's components heavily rely on existing techniques, with limited evidence of novel analytical methods tailored specifically for agentic systems.
    *   The novelty lies primarily in the application and integration of existing techniques, not the creation of fundamentally new ones.
    *   Missing a rigorous comparison against existing AIOps frameworks, highlighting its unique advantages and disadvantages.
    *   Limited discussion of the practical complexities involved in implementing AgentOps in real-world scenarios (e.g., data privacy, security, resource constraints).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting a more structured and automated approach to managing agentic AI systems. It could serve as a starting point for further research and development in this area, particularly in creating specific tools and techniques to support the AgentOps framework. However, its influence will depend on its adoption by the community and the availability of supporting tools and resources. The lack of empirical results currently limits its immediate impact.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a valuable conceptual framework for addressing the operational challenges of agentic AI systems. While the core ideas are promising, the paper falls short of presenting significant technical innovations or empirical validations. The AgentOps framework is a logical and structured approach, but its novelty is incremental, largely relying on existing techniques from other fields. The lack of empirical evidence and comparative analysis weakens the paper's immediate impact and significance. Despite these weaknesses, the paper contributes to the growing discussion surrounding the operationalization of AI systems and sets the stage for future work in this area. A score of 6 reflects the paper's moderate novelty and significance, balanced against its limitations in empirical validation and technical innovation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Ocean Diviner: A Diffusion-Augmented Reinforcement Learning for AUV Robust Control in the Underwater Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11283v1)
- **Authors**: Weiyi Liu, Jingzehua Xu, Guanwen Xie, Yi Li
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a diffusion-augmented reinforcement learning (RL) approach for robust autonomous underwater vehicle (AUV) control, addressing key challenges in underwater trajectory planning and dynamic environment adaptation. The proposed method integrates three core innovations: (1) A diffusion-based trajectory generation framework that produces physically feasible multi-step trajectories, enhanced by a high-dimensional state encoding mechanism combining current observations with historical states and actions through a novel diffusion U-Net architecture, significantly improving long-horizon planning. (2) A sample-efficient hybrid learning architecture that synergizes diffusion-guided exploration with RL policy optimization, where the diffusion model generates diverse candidate actions and the RL critic selects optimal actions, achieving higher exploration efficiency and policy stability in dynamic underwater environments. Extensive simulation experiments validating the method's superior robustness and flexibility, outperforms conventional control methods in challenging marine conditions, offering enhanced adaptability and reliability for AUV operations in the underwater tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Ocean Diviner: A Diffusion-Augmented Reinforcement Learning for AUV Robust Control in Underwater Tasks" proposes a novel approach to autonomous underwater vehicle (AUV) control. The method integrates diffusion models with reinforcement learning (RL) to address challenges like complex hydrodynamics, time-varying disturbances, and localization uncertainty. The key innovations are: (1) a diffusion-based trajectory generation framework using a U-Net architecture to produce physically feasible trajectories, (2) a hybrid learning architecture that uses the diffusion model for exploration and an RL critic for action selection, improving exploration efficiency and policy stability.  Simulation results demonstrate superior robustness and flexibility compared to traditional control methods in challenging marine environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of diffusion models with reinforcement learning for AUV control is relatively novel. While both diffusion models and RL have been applied to robotics separately, their synergistic use for robust underwater control is a distinct contribution. The U-Net architecture specifically designed for this purpose enhances its value.

*   **Significance:** AUV control in dynamic underwater environments is a challenging problem with practical implications. The paper makes a significant stride in improving robustness and adaptability in such environments. This is achieved by:

    *   Addressing the limitations of traditional control (PID, MPC, SMC) regarding adaptability and learning from experience.
    *   Overcoming sample inefficiency, and the myopic nature of conventional RL.
    *   Leveraging the strengths of diffusion models for trajectory generation and exploration, while retaining the decision-making capabilities of RL.
    *   Demonstrating, through simulations, improved performance over standard RL, especially under harsh conditions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of AUV control and motivates the proposed solution.
    *   **Technical soundness:** The approach is well-described, with clear explanations of the diffusion model, RL framework, and their integration.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The simulation experiments are thorough, comparing the proposed method against baselines and evaluating performance under different marine conditions and control configurations. Metrics like SDR, EC, and SSN offer a holistic picture of performance.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The figures and tables are well-designed and aid in understanding the results. The architectural components and algorithms are clearly explained.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulation only:** The paper lacks real-world experimental validation. Simulation results are promising, but the true test lies in deploying the method on a physical AUV in a realistic underwater environment.
    *   **Limited complexity of simulated scenarios:** Although the simulation includes waves and currents, a more comprehensive simulation environment that incorporates additional real-world noise, model errors, and sensor limitations would add more significant value.
    *   **Scalability discussions:** There needs to be additional focus on the computational demands required by diffusion models, and their potential scalability when faced with more complex environments or systems.
    *   **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** A detailed discussion of the sensitivity of the proposed method to the chosen hyperparameters is currently lacking.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper provides a solid foundation for future research in robust AUV control using diffusion models. The proposed hybrid learning architecture can potentially improve the performance of AUVs in various applications, including underwater inspection, exploration, and data collection. It opens up new avenues for exploration in the fields of diffusion models, RL-augmented decision making and dynamic control.

* **Reasoning for Score:**

The paper showcases a creative amalgamation of diffusion models and reinforcement learning in the niche field of AUV control. While the novelty is tangible and the simulation results are positive, the absence of real-world validation significantly limits its impact. The methodology is well-articulated and evaluated, contributing a sound technical foundation. The simulation's fidelity to complex real-world noise is a point that calls for some discussion.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Opus: A Prompt Intention Framework for Complex Workflow Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11288v1)
- **Authors**: Théo Fagnoni, Mahsun Altin, Chia En Chung, Phillip Kingston, Alan Tuning, Dana O. Mohamed, Inès Adnani
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces the Opus Prompt Intention Framework, designed to improve complex Workflow Generation with instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs). We propose an intermediate Intention Capture layer between user queries and Workflow Generation, implementing the Opus Workflow Intention Framework, which consists of extracting Workflow Signals from user queries, interpreting them into structured Workflow Intention objects, and generating Workflows based on these Intentions. Our results show that this layer enables LLMs to produce logical and meaningful outputs that scale reliably as query complexity increases. On a synthetic benchmark of 1,000 multi-intent query-Workflow(s) pairs, applying the Opus Prompt Intention Framework to Workflow Generation yields consistent improvements in semantic Workflow similarity metrics. In this paper, we introduce the Opus Prompt Intention Framework by applying the concepts of Workflow Signal and Workflow Intention to LLM-driven Workflow Generation. We present a reproducible, customizable LLM-based Intention Capture system to extract Workflow Signals and Workflow Intentions from user queries. Finally, we provide empirical evidence that the proposed system significantly improves Workflow Generation quality compared to direct generation from user queries, particularly in cases of Mixed Intention Elicitation.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces the Opus Prompt Intention Framework, designed to improve complex workflow generation using instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework proposes an intermediate "Intention Capture" layer between user queries and workflow generation, extracting workflow signals, interpreting them into structured intention objects, and then generating workflows. The paper claims this approach allows LLMs to produce more logical and meaningful outputs that scale better with query complexity, demonstrating improvements in semantic workflow similarity metrics on a synthetic benchmark. The paper presents a reproducible LLM-based intention capture system and provides empirical evidence supporting the improvement in workflow generation quality, especially for mixed intention elicitation scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using an intermediate "Intention Capture" layer is a reasonably novel approach to improving workflow generation with LLMs. While the concepts of workflow signals and intentions are not entirely new, their specific application in a framework to structure LLM prompts and guide workflow generation is a worthwhile contribution. The paper's strength lies in its concrete implementation and empirical evaluation.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in applying LLMs to real-world automation: the ability to reliably generate complex workflows from potentially ambiguous or multi-faceted user queries. The Opus Prompt Intention Framework has the potential to enhance the robustness and scalability of LLM-driven workflow generation systems. By explicitly capturing user intentions, the framework can mitigate the risk of LLMs generating inconsistent or inaccurate workflows, especially when dealing with queries that express multiple objectives. This improved reliability can significantly impact domains like Business Process Outsourcing (BPO) where automation is critical for efficiency and accuracy. However, the evaluation is only done on synthetic data, and real-world user generated queries may pose a significantly different problem.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of generating complex workflows from potentially ambiguous user queries.
*   **Well-Defined Framework:** The Opus Prompt Intention Framework is presented with sufficient detail to understand its components and implementation.
*   **Reproducible System:** The emphasis on a reproducible LLM-based intention capture system is a major plus, making the research more valuable to the community.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The use of a synthetic benchmark and various evaluation metrics (semantic similarity and LLM-as-a-judge) provides a thorough assessment of the framework's performance. The results convincingly demonstrate that the intention capture layer improves workflow generation quality. The empirical evidence, however, is limited by the fact that the data is synthetic. It is unclear that the same approach will work on real-world queries.
*   **Scalability claim:** The fact that the performance degradation as complexity increases is lower when using intention capture.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Benchmark:** The evaluation relies on a synthetic dataset. While useful for controlled experiments, it does not fully capture the nuances and complexities of real-world user queries. Further evaluation on a real-world dataset is needed to validate the framework's practical applicability.
*   **LLM-as-a-Judge limitations**: while they provide additional, qualitatively assessments of the workflow quality, they are limited by biases of the LLM doing the judging.
*   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses primarily on text-based queries. The framework's applicability to multimodal inputs (e.g., images, audio) is not explored.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** The incremental novelty, as many components are present in other systems is a potential weakness.

**Potential Influence:**

The Opus Prompt Intention Framework has the potential to influence the design of future LLM-driven workflow generation systems. The framework's modular approach and focus on explicit intention capture offer a promising path towards more reliable and scalable automation. The research findings could also encourage further exploration of intermediate representations for bridging the gap between natural language and executable systems.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with a well-defined framework, reproducible system, and promising empirical results. However, the limitations of the synthetic benchmark and the incremental novelty prevent a higher score. The potential influence of the research on the field of LLM-driven automation is significant, and the positive performance improvements shown by the framework certainly show a clear positive result, but more real-world validation is needed.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Internal Value Alignment in Large Language Models through Controlled Value Vector Activation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11316v1)
- **Authors**: Haoran Jin, Meng Li, Xiting Wang, Zhihao Xu, Minlie Huang, Yantao Jia, Defu Lian
- **Abstract**: Aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human values has attracted increasing attention since it provides clarity, transparency, and the ability to adapt to evolving scenarios. In this paper, we introduce a Controlled Value Vector Activation (ConVA) method that directly aligns the internal values of LLMs by interpreting how a value is encoded in their latent representations and modifies relevant activations to ensure consistent values in LLMs. To ensure an accurate and unbiased interpretation, we propose a context-controlled value vector identification method. To consistently control values without sacrificing model performance, we introduce a gated value vector activation method for effective and minimum degree of value control. Experiments show that our method achieves the highest control success rate across 10 basic values without hurting LLM performance and fluency, and ensures target values even with opposite and potentially malicious input prompts. Source code and data are available at~ https://github.com/hr-jin/ConVA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ConVA (Controlled Value Vector Activation), a framework designed to align large language models (LLMs) with human values by directly manipulating the model's internal representations.  ConVA identifies value vectors within the LLM's latent space using a context-controlled method to minimize contextual biases in the identification process. It then employs a gated activation mechanism to modify relevant activations, ensuring value consistency without significantly impacting the model's performance and general capabilities. The paper presents experiments demonstrating that ConVA achieves high control success rates across ten basic values without substantially compromising LLM fluency and performance, even in the face of adversarial prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a novel approach to value alignment by operating directly on the LLM's internal activations, rather than relying solely on behavior-level techniques like SFT or RLHF. The key contributions lie in:

1.  **Context-Controlled Value Vector Identification:**  This is a relatively novel contribution, addressing a key weakness in existing activation engineering techniques.  The use of paired positive and negative samples, carefully designed to isolate the value-independent context, appears to be effective at reducing bias. This is a clear improvement over simply prompting the model to generate value-aligned data. The inclusion of a user study to validate the dataset quality adds credibility.

2.  **Gated Value Vector Activation:** The gated mechanism is a clever way to balance value control with model performance. This is not entirely novel in activation engineering in general, but its application to value alignment and the specific optimization approach used in the paper are unique.

**Significance:**  Value alignment is a crucial challenge for LLMs, and the paper addresses a key limitation of existing approaches (black-box nature and lack of interpretability).  The ability to directly influence the internal value representations of an LLM has the potential to increase transparency and control over its behavior. Furthermore, demonstrating that this can be done without significantly degrading general capabilities is significant. The experiments are comprehensive, comparing against several strong baselines and demonstrating generalizability across different LLM architectures. The analysis of the value structure within the LLMs is also a valuable insight.

**Weaknesses:**

1.  **Dependence on GPT-4o for Dataset Generation:**  While the context control methodology improves dataset quality, it still relies on another LLM (GPT-4o) to generate training data.  This raises questions about the inherent biases present in GPT-4o and how those might influence the learned value vectors. While the human evaluation is a good step, a deeper investigation of the dataset's limitations is needed.

2.  **Reliance on Schwartz's Theory:** The paper adopts Schwartz's theory as a predefined set of values. While widely accepted, this is still one particular value framework. The generalizability of ConVA to other value theories is not explicitly explored. It could also be that this theory isn't how values are organized in LLMs.

3.  **Empirical Validation of Value Alignment:** The evaluation heavily relies on LLM-based metrics (assessing control success and fluency). Although validated with a user study, a broader set of evaluation methods, perhaps including scenarios involving real-world consequences, would further strengthen the claims.

4. **Gate Performance and Alternative Gating:** The gate unit is essentially a value detector. The paper needs to better justify its choice of DeBERTa and explain why it’s a solid way to detect value-related inputs. Further, while using a gate is beneficial, the trade-off between model utility and protecting capabilities warrants further investigation. The paper should discuss whether other gating mechanisms exist (e.g., thresholding the model's own value vector output).

5. **Limited Discussion on Potential Misuse:** While the ethical statement is present, the paper could delve deeper into the potential for misuse. Specifically, the ability to control internal representations could be used to enforce harmful biases or manipulate LLMs for malicious purposes.

**Potential Influence:**  If the techniques presented are robust and scalable, ConVA could influence the development of more transparent and controllable LLMs. The methodology for context-controlled value vector identification could become a standard practice in activation engineering.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contributions to the field, along with some limitations. The context-controlled value vector identification and gated activation mechanism are significant advancements in the field. The comprehensive experiments and analysis provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the ConVA framework.

However, the paper's reliance on GPT-4o for dataset generation, predefined value frameworks, and LLM-based evaluation raises some concerns. Also, while the gate is a good initial approach, there may be other methods to achieve the same goal that have better trade-offs. While the potential for misuse is mentioned, the paper could further explore these issues. Addressing these weaknesses in future work would significantly increase the paper's impact and move towards a more reliable system.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Guiding LLM Decision-Making with Fairness Reward Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11344v1)
- **Authors**: Zara Hall, Melanie Subbiah, Thomas P Zollo, Kathleen McKeown, Richard Zemel
- **Abstract**: Large language models are increasingly used to support high-stakes decisions, potentially influencing who is granted bail or receives a loan. Naive chain-of-thought sampling can improve average decision accuracy, but has also been shown to amplify unfair bias. To address this challenge and enable the trustworthy use of reasoning models in high-stakes decision-making, we propose a framework for training a generalizable Fairness Reward Model (FRM). Our model assigns a fairness score to LLM reasoning, enabling the system to down-weight biased trajectories and favor equitable ones when aggregating decisions across reasoning chains. We show that a single Fairness Reward Model, trained on weakly supervised, LLM-annotated examples of biased versus unbiased reasoning, transfers across tasks, domains, and model families without additional fine-tuning. Applied to real-world decision-making tasks including recidivism prediction and social media moderation, we show that our approach consistently improves fairness while matching, or even surpassing, baseline accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Guiding LLM Decision-Making with Fairness Reward Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework using Fairness Reward Models (FRMs) to mitigate bias in LLMs used for decision-making. The core idea is to train an FRM to assign fairness scores to LLM reasoning steps, allowing the system to down-weight biased trajectories and favor equitable ones when aggregating decisions across reasoning chains. A single FRM, trained on weakly supervised LLM-annotated examples, demonstrates transferability across tasks, domains, and model families. The approach improves fairness in real-world decision-making tasks, such as recidivism prediction and social media moderation, while maintaining or surpassing baseline accuracy. The paper shows improvements across multiple dimensions of fairness like race, religion, and gender and compares to existing baseline approaches. The method scores reasoning chains after their creation, which enables control of fairness/accuracy trade-offs, is novel and previously unachieved via fine-tuning approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to addressing bias in LLMs by focusing on the reasoning process rather than just the output. The use of a generalizable FRM, trained with weak supervision, and its demonstrated transferability across diverse tasks and models is a significant contribution. The approach is novel for how well the FRM transfers across a variety of downstream tasks and also for avoiding finetuning or other approaches that involve potentially altering the original model.

*   **Significance:** The problem of bias in LLMs is critical, especially in high-stakes decision-making scenarios.  The paper provides a practical and scalable solution that can improve fairness without sacrificing accuracy. The potential impact is substantial, as it offers a way to deploy more trustworthy reasoning models in various applications, including criminal justice, content moderation, and hiring.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Generalizability:** The FRM demonstrates impressive generalization capabilities across different tasks, domains, reasoning models, and protected attributes.
    *   **Weak Supervision:** The method relies on weakly supervised labels generated by LLMs, making it more scalable than approaches requiring human annotation.
    *   **Accuracy and Fairness:**  The framework consistently improves fairness while maintaining or even improving accuracy, addressing a common trade-off in fairness interventions.
    *   **Transparent and Auditable:** The step-localized fairness scores make the decision-making process more transparent and auditable, allowing practitioners to trace unfair outcomes to specific lines of reasoning.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation across diverse real-world datasets and demonstrates significant improvements over strong baselines. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the design decisions of the framework.
    *   **Adaptability:** Method enables the end-user to make fairness/accuracy trade-off decisions and has potential for integration into reinforcement learning methods in the future.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM-Annotated Labels:** The FRM's performance is contingent on the quality of the weakly supervised labels generated by LLMs. The paper acknowledges that these labels may contain biases of their own. While a small human study validates the quality of these labels, further investigation is warranted.
    *   **Limited Scope of Fairness Metrics:** The paper primarily evaluates fairness using equalized odds and equalized opportunity. While these metrics are widely used, they may not capture all aspects of fairness. Exploring other fairness notions, such as calibration within groups, could provide a more comprehensive assessment.

    *   **Potential for Over-correction:** Down-weighting reasoning steps deemed "biased" could inadvertently suppress legitimate but unconventional reasoning, especially if the FRM is overly sensitive to mentions of protected attributes. While the quantitative results suggest this isn't a major issue, it's a potential concern.
    *   **Context Dependence:** While mentioned in the limitations, context-dependent annotation as well as the applicability of FRM across languages could be explored further.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of responsible AI. It provides a practical and scalable solution for mitigating bias in LLMs, making them more trustworthy for decision-making applications. The framework can be extended and adapted to various other tasks and domains. The focus on the *reasoning process* represents a useful shift in thinking about addressing bias in LLMs and is an important contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

This is a very strong paper, achieving a high degree of novelty, a scalable solution, and a significant impact on the area of AI trustworthiness. It has some limitations (reliance on LLM labels, scope of fairness metrics), which are acknowledged by the authors, but the strengths outweigh these drawbacks. The extensive experiments are very supportive and it is likely to motivate the larger research community to further improve fairness of AI.

### Foundation Models for Logistics: Toward Certifiable, Conversational Planning Interfaces
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11352v1)
- **Authors**: Yunhao Yang, Neel P. Bhatt, Christian Ellis, Alvaro Velasquez, Zhangyang Wang, Ufuk Topcu
- **Abstract**: Logistics operators, from battlefield coordinators rerouting airlifts ahead of a storm to warehouse managers juggling late trucks, often face life-critical decisions that demand both domain expertise and rapid and continuous replanning. While popular methods like integer programming yield logistics plans that satisfy user-defined logical constraints, they are slow and assume an idealized mathematical model of the environment that does not account for uncertainty. On the other hand, large language models (LLMs) can handle uncertainty and promise to accelerate replanning while lowering the barrier to entry by translating free-form utterances into executable plans, yet they remain prone to misinterpretations and hallucinations that jeopardize safety and cost. We introduce a neurosymbolic framework that pairs the accessibility of natural-language dialogue with verifiable guarantees on goal interpretation. It converts user requests into structured planning specifications, quantifies its own uncertainty at the field and token level, and invokes an interactive clarification loop whenever confidence falls below an adaptive threshold. A lightweight model, fine-tuned on just 100 uncertainty-filtered examples, surpasses the zero-shot performance of GPT-4.1 while cutting inference latency by nearly 50%. These preliminary results highlight a practical path toward certifiable, real-time, and user-aligned decision-making for complex logistics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a vision for "Vision-Language-Logistics agents" (VLL agents) aimed at creating conversational and certifiable planning interfaces for logistics. These agents would leverage multimodal AI to understand free-form instructions, ground them in real-time perceptual data, synthesize executable plans, and provide explicit uncertainty signals and formal checks to ensure alignment with the user's intent. The core contribution is a neurosymbolic framework featuring an uncertainty-aware intent-verification loop that interacts with the user to clarify ambiguous requests, enhancing the reliability of the extracted goals. The paper presents preliminary results on a cargo-routing benchmark showing that uncertainty-filtered fine-tuning can improve both accuracy and responsiveness compared to relying solely on large language models (LLMs).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for logistics planning interfaces is not entirely novel.  Many prior works have explored using LLMs to translate natural language into structured planning representations. However, the focus on explicitly quantifying and addressing *uncertainty* in goal understanding, and then integrating that with a clarification loop, appears to be a more original contribution. The idea of learning from interactions to improve the LLM is also valuable. The paper builds on existing ideas in uncertainty estimation and neurosymbolic AI, but the specific combination applied to logistics and conversational interfaces seems relatively fresh.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of VLL agents is significant. Improving the accessibility and reliability of logistics planning tools could be extremely valuable in high-stakes scenarios, such as disaster relief or supply chain management. The framework addresses a crucial limitation of directly applying LLMs to these tasks: their tendency to hallucinate or misinterpret user intent.  The work aligns with the broader trend of making AI systems more transparent and trustworthy by providing uncertainty estimates and incorporating human feedback.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of ensuring reliable goal understanding in logistics planning.
    *   **Clear architecture:** The neurosymbolic architecture and the uncertainty-aware intent-verification loop are well-explained.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The preliminary results demonstrate the potential of the approach. The reduction in inference latency and the improvement in accuracy are both promising.
    *   **Focus on practicality:**  The paper emphasizes building a system that can be integrated into existing logistics pipelines.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of evaluation:** The evaluation is confined to a cargo-routing benchmark. The generalizability of the results to other logistics domains and different types of uncertainty remains unclear. The paper claims to surpass the zero-shot performance of GPT-4.1 with a much smaller model, but the dataset size (100 labeled examples) and type are not rigorous enough to be persuasive.
    *   **Lack of detail on vision integration:** The "vision" aspect of the VLL agent is only mentioned briefly, without any details on how visual data is used to ground user instructions. This feels like a significant omission.
    *   **Limited comparison to baselines:**  It's not clear how the proposed approach compares to more traditional methods for handling uncertainty in planning, such as robust optimization or stochastic programming.
    *   **Modest improvement:** Performance compared to existing models is not drastically improved. The model fine-tuned with 100 labeled data instances achieved an edge over other models. This leaves a question of scalability and practical applicability of the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the framework can be scaled and generalized, it could influence the design of AI-powered planning systems in other domains besides logistics.  The emphasis on uncertainty estimation and human-in-the-loop validation could become a standard practice for high-stakes applications of LLMs.

Overall, while the paper has promise, its novelty and current validation are not revolutionary. The work builds logically and sensibly on existing ideas, but the impact hinges on its generalizability and wider evaluation.
