- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### What is the Best Process Model Representation? A Comparative Analysis for Process Modeling with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11356v1)
- **Authors**: Alexis Brissard, Frédéric Cuppens, Amal Zouaq
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly applied for Process Modeling (PMo) tasks such as Process Model Generation (PMG). To support these tasks, researchers have introduced a variety of Process Model Representations (PMRs) that serve as model abstractions or generation targets. However, these PMRs differ widely in structure, complexity, and usability, and have never been systematically compared. Moreover, recent PMG approaches rely on distinct evaluation strategies and generation techniques, making comparison difficult. This paper presents the first empirical study that evaluates multiple PMRs in the context of PMo with LLMs. We introduce the PMo Dataset, a new dataset containing 55 process descriptions paired with models in nine different PMRs. We evaluate PMRs along two dimensions: suitability for LLM-based PMo and performance on PMG. \textit{Mermaid} achieves the highest overall score across six PMo criteria, whereas \textit{BPMN text} delivers the best PMG results in terms of process element similarity.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, along with a score.

**Summary:**

The paper "What is the Best Process Model Representation? A Comparative Analysis for Process Modeling with Large Language Models" addresses the lack of systematic comparison of Process Model Representations (PMRs) when used with Large Language Models (LLMs) for Process Modeling (PMo) tasks, particularly Process Model Generation (PMG). The authors introduce the PMo Dataset, containing 55 process descriptions paired with models in nine different PMRs. They evaluate PMRs based on their suitability for LLM-based PMo (considering factors like token compactness, expressiveness, readability, visualization, usability, and extensibility) and performance on PMG (using element counts and PME similarity as metrics).  The results indicate that Mermaid performs well overall for PMo tasks due to its compactness and visualizability. However, BPMN text achieves better performance on PMG concerning process element similarity, suggesting that different PMRs might be suitable for different stages of PMo pipelines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive comparative analysis of various PMRs. While LLMs have been used for PMo before, this study is, to the best of my knowledge, the first to systematically compare a diverse set of PMRs, both conceptually and empirically, providing a valuable contribution to the field. The introduction of the PMo Dataset is also significant as it provides a unified benchmark for future research. The idea of leveraging different PMRs during different PMo pipeline stages is a notable insight.

*   **Significance:** The findings have several important implications. First, it provides guidance for researchers and practitioners in selecting appropriate PMRs for specific PMo tasks. The identified strengths and weaknesses of each PMR enable more informed decision-making. Second, the PMo Dataset facilitates reproducible research and allows for standardized comparisons of new PMG approaches. Third, the insight about the possible advantages of using different PMRs during different parts of the overall PMo pipeline could lead to more sophisticated and effective PMo systems. Finally, the study highlights the limitations of current LLMs in fully capturing process semantics, particularly concerning gateway generation, which motivates future research directions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a detailed analysis across multiple dimensions, including conceptual analysis of PMRs, quantitative evaluation of length, and experimental evaluation of PMG performance.
    *   **Well-Defined Criteria:** The six requirements defined for PMRs in the context of LLM-based PMo are clearly articulated and justified.
    *   **Unified Benchmark:** The PMo Dataset addresses the issue of fragmented evaluation methods in the field, enabling more reliable comparisons.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide access to their code, data, and prompts, facilitating reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Subjectivity in PMo evaluation:** Human readability, visualizations, usability and extensibility are graded by the authors and this is inevitably subjective.
    *   **Limited Process Elements:** The PMG experiments focus on a simplified set of process elements, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world process models. The study could be extended to include more advanced process elements.
    *   **Single LLM Evaluation:** The experiments are conducted using only one LLM (LLaMA-3-70b). While the choice of an open-source LLM is commendable for reproducibility, evaluating with other models might reveal different performance characteristics of PMRs.
    *   **Standard Prompting only:** Only standard prompting is explored and no other techniques are tested like fine-tuning.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by providing a foundation for future research on PMo with LLMs. The PMo Dataset will likely become a valuable resource for benchmarking new approaches. The insights gained from the comparative analysis can guide the development of more effective PMo systems.

*   **Justification of the Score:** Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field. It has clear novelty, provides valuable insights, and offers a new benchmark dataset. While the evaluation isn't fully comprehensive, and is subjective in parts, the methodological approach and the results are significant enough to justify a high score. The paper clearly addresses a gap in current research, with valuable practical implications.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### From Chaos to Automation: Enabling the Use of Unstructured Data for Robotic Process Automation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11364v1)
- **Authors**: Kelly Kurowski, Xixi Lu, Hajo A. Reijers
- **Abstract**: The growing volume of unstructured data within organizations poses significant challenges for data analysis and process automation. Unstructured data, which lacks a predefined format, encompasses various forms such as emails, reports, and scans. It is estimated to constitute approximately 80% of enterprise data. Despite the valuable insights it can offer, extracting meaningful information from unstructured data is more complex compared to structured data. Robotic Process Automation (RPA) has gained popularity for automating repetitive tasks, improving efficiency, and reducing errors. However, RPA is traditionally reliant on structured data, limiting its application to processes involving unstructured documents. This study addresses this limitation by developing the UNstructured Document REtrieval SyStem (UNDRESS), a system that uses fuzzy regular expressions, techniques for natural language processing, and large language models to enable RPA platforms to effectively retrieve information from unstructured documents. The research involved the design and development of a prototype system, and its subsequent evaluation based on text extraction and information retrieval performance. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of UNDRESS in enhancing RPA capabilities for unstructured data, providing a significant advancement in the field. The findings suggest that this system could facilitate broader RPA adoption across processes traditionally hindered by unstructured data, thereby improving overall business process efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UNstructured Document Retrieval SyStem (UNDRESS), a system designed to enhance Robotic Process Automation (RPA) by enabling it to effectively process unstructured data.  UNDRESS uses a hybrid approach, combining fuzzy regular expressions, Named Entity Recognition (NER), and Large Language Models (LLMs) to extract and retrieve information from documents. The system is designed with interchangeable components, allowing for customization and adaptation.  The paper evaluates UNDRESS on two datasets (real invoices and generated resumes) and conducts interviews with RPA developers to assess its practical relevance. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of UNDRESS in improving RPA capabilities for unstructured data, offering a customizable and potentially more sustainable alternative to sole reliance on LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual techniques (fuzzy regular expressions, NER, and LLMs) are well-established, the *integration* of these techniques into a single, customizable framework tailored explicitly for RPA's unstructured data challenge is the primary contribution. The key novelty lies in the hybrid architecture and the modular design, allowing for adaptation and avoiding over-reliance on computationally intensive and less controllable LLMs. The novelty of the solution is somewhat limited, since related work has already explored integrating these components for similar unstructured data processing, however specifically in the RPA context makes it a contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real and significant problem in RPA – its reliance on structured data and the difficulty of processing unstructured documents which is often seen as a barrier to the wider adoption of the technology. By enabling RPA to work with unstructured data, UNDRESS can potentially streamline a wider range of business processes, increasing efficiency and reducing errors. The inclusion of RPA developers as a user group for early-stage evaluation is a strength and a strong indicator of the relevance of the research. The quantitative evaluation clearly shows that UNDRESS improves the extraction of information and provides relevant extraction compared to simpler approaches.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on a practical problem in RPA with a clear business impact enhances the paper's value.
    *   **Hybrid Approach:** The combination of techniques offers flexibility and potentially greater robustness than relying on a single approach. The work explicitly investigates the tradeoff between the high-powered LLMs vs. more interpretable simple algorithms like regular expressions.
    *   **Modularity:** The system's modular design allows for customization and adaptation to specific use cases.
    *   **Evaluation:** The use of two distinct datasets and qualitative feedback from RPA developers provides a comprehensive evaluation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the hybrid approach has merit, the gains over simpler solutions (regular expressions) for certain fields are not always substantial, potentially questioning the need for more complex and expensive LLMs in every case.
    *   **Lack of comparison:** While the quantitative results are presented, it would have been beneficial to include a direct comparison against a *state-of-the-art* baseline RPA system that *only* uses LLMs (or other existing integrated RPA platforms).
    *   **Limited Scope**:  The study is limited to invoices and resumes. While they are relevant, expanding to other common document types would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Explainable AI for the component-level:** The paper does not touch on the possibility of introducing Explainable AI (XAI) techniques to evaluate each of the models. This could have added additional clarity on *when* which model is the best to use, or the trade offs between these models.

* **Future Directions:** The paper proposes future research directions and the directions provided seem very promising such as using the context and spatial analysis to improve the alignment of labels or entities and how to improve the handling of duplicates.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a practical and well-motivated system for improving RPA's ability to handle unstructured data. While the core techniques are not entirely novel, the hybrid architecture, modular design, and practical evaluation contribute to the field. The evaluation demonstrates clear improvements in some areas. Overall, the work makes a useful contribution to the field.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Step-wise Policy for Rare-tool Knowledge (SPaRK): Offline RL that Drives Diverse Tool Use in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11371v1)
- **Authors**: Gabriel Bo, Koa Chang, Justin Gu
- **Abstract**: We present Step-wise Policy for Rare-tool Knowledge (SPaRK), a novel reinforcement learning framework that teaches large language models to explore diverse tool usage patterns beyond conventional high-temperature sampling. Building on recent advances in step-wise reinforcement learning, we introduce a dual-objective reward system that simultaneously optimizes for answer quality and tool diversity, training a Llama-3.1 8B model through offline PPO on synthetically generated trajectories from the MMLU-Pro dataset. Our approach uniquely employs a rarity-first exploitation strategy where a GPT-4o judge scores candidate actions across eight distinct tools plus chain-of-thought reasoning, with the policy favoring less-frequently used but still viable tools to encourage systematic exploration. Empirical results demonstrate that SPaRK achieves competitive performance across 14 MMLU-Pro categories while exhibiting significantly higher entropy in tool selection compared to both baseline and supervised fine-tuning approaches, suggesting that algorithmic exploration through explicit tool diversity can enhance reasoning capabilities without sacrificing accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Step-wise Policy for Rare-tool Knowledge (SPARK): Offline RL that Drives Diverse Tool Use in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPARK, a novel reinforcement learning framework designed to enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by encouraging the use of diverse tools. SPARK models tool selection as a learnable policy within a multi-step Markov Decision Process, incorporating a composite reward function that incentivizes both answer correctness and tool diversity. It uses a rarity-first exploitation strategy, favoring less-frequently used but viable tools to promote systematic exploration. The framework trains a Llama-3.1 8B model using offline Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) on synthetically generated trajectories from the MMLU-Pro dataset. The results demonstrate that SPARK achieves higher accuracy and significantly higher entropy in tool selection compared to baseline and supervised fine-tuning approaches, suggesting that algorithmic exploration through explicit tool diversity can enhance reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Approach:** SPARK offers a novel approach to tool utilization in LLMs by explicitly modeling tool selection as a learnable policy and incentivizing tool diversity, moving beyond brute-force sampling or temperature-based exploration.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitation of current approaches that rely heavily on high-frequency tools, hindering the exploration of the full spectrum of available resources.
    *   **Effective Methodology:** The rarity-first exploitation strategy is a clever way to balance exploration and exploitation, encouraging the use of less frequent but viable tools.
    *   **Promising Results:** The experimental results demonstrate a significant improvement in accuracy and tool diversity compared to baseline and supervised fine-tuning approaches.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation covers various model variants and provides quantitative and qualitative analysis of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Baseline:** The lack of a critical baseline—the base model augmented with tool access but without specialized training—limits the strength of the claims. It's difficult to definitively attribute the performance gains to the learned exploration policy rather than just tool availability. This is a major weakness.
    *   **Synthetic Data Dependency:** The framework relies on synthetically generated trajectories, which may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world scenarios. The quality of the judge LLM is also important here.
    *   **Offline PPO:** The use of offline PPO may limit the adaptability of the policy compared to online PPO training.
    *   **Resource Constraints:** The authors acknowledge resource constraints as a limitation, hindering a more thorough investigation of variations in the diversity coefficient and the impact of different judge models.
    *   **MMLU-Pro benchmark limitations:** The MMLU-Pro benchmark may be easier than AIMO which limits the strength of the claims.

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to tool selection in LLMs by explicitly modeling it as a learnable policy and incentivizing tool diversity. The rarity-first exploitation strategy is also a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings suggest that algorithmic exploration through explicit tool diversity can significantly enhance reasoning capabilities in LLMs. This has important implications for the development of more capable and adaptable language agents. The identified limitations and suggested directions for future work provide valuable insights for researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to tool utilization in LLMs, with clear problem definition, effective methodology, and encouraging results. However, the lack of a critical baseline and reliance on synthetic data limit the strength of the claims. The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of algorithmic exploration through explicit tool diversity, but it also acknowledges important limitations and suggests directions for future work. It is also limited to the MMLU-Pro benchmark.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### DCR: Quantifying Data Contamination in LLMs Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11405v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng Xu, Nan Yan, Shuhao Guan, Changhong Jin, Yuke Mei, Yibing Guo, M-Tahar Kechadi
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has heightened concerns about benchmark data contamination (BDC), where models inadvertently memorize evaluation data, inflating performance metrics and undermining genuine generalization assessment. This paper introduces the Data Contamination Risk (DCR) framework, a lightweight, interpretable pipeline designed to detect and quantify BDC across four granular levels: semantic, informational, data, and label. By synthesizing contamination scores via a fuzzy inference system, DCR produces a unified DCR Factor that adjusts raw accuracy to reflect contamination-aware performance. Validated on 9 LLMs (0.5B-72B) across sentiment analysis, fake news detection, and arithmetic reasoning tasks, the DCR framework reliably diagnoses contamination severity and with accuracy adjusted using the DCR Factor to within 4% average error across the three benchmarks compared to the uncontaminated baseline. Emphasizing computational efficiency and transparency, DCR provides a practical tool for integrating contamination assessment into routine evaluations, fostering fairer comparisons and enhancing the credibility of LLM benchmarking practices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DCR paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Data Contamination Risk (DCR), a framework for detecting and quantifying benchmark data contamination (BDC) in Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework operates at four granular levels (semantic, informational, data, and label) and uses a fuzzy inference system to generate a DCR Factor, which then adjusts the raw accuracy of LLMs to reflect contamination-aware performance.  The framework is tested on several LLMs of varying sizes (0.5B-72B) across sentiment analysis, fake news detection, and arithmetic reasoning tasks. The authors claim the DCR framework can reliably diagnose contamination severity and provide accuracy adjustments within 4% average error compared to uncontaminated baselines.  DCR emphasizes computational efficiency and interpretability, making it a practical tool for integrating contamination assessment into routine LLM evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The *idea* of detecting benchmark contamination is not new; however, the paper presents a somewhat novel *framework* for achieving this. The combination of multi-level analysis with a fuzzy inference system is a differentiating factor. The four levels of analysis (Semantic, Information, Data, and Label) provide a structured approach, and the use of a fuzzy system for aggregation of contamination scores is reasonably innovative. The introduction of DCR Factor for adjusted accuracy is welcome as it provides a single metric to reflect contamination level.

*   **Significance:** Addressing benchmark contamination is crucial for the reliable evaluation and comparison of LLMs. The paper's focus on computational efficiency and interpretability increases the practical applicability of the proposed framework. It attempts to bridge the gap between research and real-world LLM development, where complex and resource-intensive BDC mitigation techniques are not always adopted. The claim that DCR can be integrated into routine evaluations is important.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Focus:** The framework is explicitly designed to be computationally efficient and easily integrated into existing evaluation pipelines.
    *   **Interpretability:** DCR provides clear explanations of BDC risk based on its analysis levels, making it easier for developers to understand and address the issues.
    *   **Multi-Level Analysis:** Analyzing contamination at different granularities is more robust than relying on simple token matching.
    *   **DCR Factor Metric:** The proposed metric adjusts the performance metrics in order to reflect real generalization capabilities.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** A range of model sizes and tasks were used for testing.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Manual Check Step:** The reliance on manual analysis (the check step) is a significant bottleneck in the DCR workflow and it is highly subjective. The paper mentions model-assisted methods as a possible alternative, but in its current form, DCR does not scale well without significant manual effort. Although they test a model to assist with the check step, it is unreliable.
    *   **Limited Model Variety:**  The study primarily relies on open-source models, which might limit the generalizability of the findings to closed-source, frontier-scale LLMs.
    *   **Simulated Contamination:** Contamination injection can only *approximate* real-world BDC, and the specific injection methodologies could influence the results.
    *   **Fuzzy System Tuning:** The fuzzy system settings were not carefully adjusted for each benchmark. This suggests that the DCR framework is not fine-tuned to achieve the best results.
    *   **Subjectivity and Definition:** The definition of BDC is subjective and can be a source of controversy. How to correctly measure the contamination is another complex task as models are trained on a corpus of data for knowledge retrieval, rather than information retrieval.

*   **Potential Influence:** The DCR framework has the potential to encourage more transparent and reliable LLM benchmarking practices. If adopted, it could lead to more accurate and fair comparisons between different models and highlight the importance of controlling for BDC in future LLM development.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper provides a valuable contribution to the important problem of benchmark data contamination. The DCR framework is reasonably novel and has the potential to be a practical tool for LLM developers. However, the dependency on manual analysis, a limited model variety, and the use of simulated contamination are major weaknesses. While the DCR Factor is a welcome addition, the lack of fine-tuning limits the generalizablity of the metric.

**Score: 6**

*   **Rationale:** The framework is reasonably innovative, practical, and contributes to an important area of research. However, the strong reliance on manual steps significantly limits its scalability and impact. The lack of fine-tuning also restricts the application of DCR.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### EXAONE 4.0: Unified Large Language Models Integrating Non-reasoning and Reasoning Modes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11407v1)
- **Authors**: LG AI Research, :, Kyunghoon Bae, Eunbi Choi, Kibong Choi, Stanley Jungkyu Choi, Yemuk Choi, Kyubeen Han, Seokhee Hong, Junwon Hwang, Taewan Hwang, Joonwon Jang, Hyojin Jeon, Kijeong Jeon, Gerrard Jeongwon Jo, Hyunjik Jo, Jiyeon Jung, Euisoon Kim, Hyosang Kim, Jihoon Kim, Joonkee Kim, Seonghwan Kim, Soyeon Kim, Sunkyoung Kim, Yireun Kim, Yongil Kim, Youchul Kim, Edward Hwayoung Lee, Gwangho Lee, Haeju Lee, Honglak Lee, Jinsik Lee, Kyungmin Lee, Sangha Park, Young Min Paik, Yongmin Park, Youngyong Park, Sanghyun Seo, Sihoon Yang, Heuiyeen Yeen, Sihyuk Yi, Hyeongu Yun
- **Abstract**: This technical report introduces EXAONE 4.0, which integrates a Non-reasoning mode and a Reasoning mode to achieve both the excellent usability of EXAONE 3.5 and the advanced reasoning abilities of EXAONE Deep. To pave the way for the agentic AI era, EXAONE 4.0 incorporates essential features such as agentic tool use, and its multilingual capabilities are extended to support Spanish in addition to English and Korean. The EXAONE 4.0 model series consists of two sizes: a mid-size 32B model optimized for high performance, and a small-size 1.2B model designed for on-device applications. The EXAONE 4.0 demonstrates superior performance compared to open-weight models in its class and remains competitive even against frontier-class models. The models are publicly available for research purposes and can be easily downloaded via https://huggingface.co/LGAI-EXAONE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the EXAONE 4.0 paper:

**Summary:**

The EXAONE 4.0 technical report introduces an updated large language model series from LG AI Research.  The key advancements focus on integrating both a non-reasoning and a reasoning mode within a single model. This is intended to combine the usability of EXAONE 3.5 with the advanced reasoning capabilities of EXAONE Deep. It also adds agentic tool use capabilities and expands multilingual support to include Spanish.  The EXAONE 4.0 series consists of two models (32B and 1.2B), optimized for performance and on-device applications, respectively. The models utilize a hybrid attention mechanism and repositioned layer normalization. The paper presents benchmark results demonstrating competitive performance against other open-weight models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The integration of non-reasoning and reasoning modes within a single model is a valuable feature.  While not entirely groundbreaking, it streamlines the user experience and potentially allows for more versatile applications. The agentic tool use incorporation is timely, as it aligns with the growing interest in AI agents. The hybrid attention mechanism and modified layer normalization, although based on prior research, are non-trivial architectural adjustments that improve the trade-off between performance and efficiency, as does the careful data selection and the progressive training recipe. However, the novelty of these individual components (hybrid attention and repositioned layer norm) on their own isn't high; they are more about application and refinement of existing techniques within a new model.

* **Significance:**  The main significance lies in providing a readily available, competitive open-weight model. The expansion to Spanish is a positive step toward broader accessibility. The competitive benchmark results demonstrate the model's potential for various real-world applications. Making the models publicly available for research is a significant contribution to the community and enables further exploration and development by other researchers.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Combines usability and reasoning: The integration of two modes is a practical design choice.
    *   Agentic AI Focus: Introduction of agentic tool use is forward-thinking.
    *   Multilingual Expansion: Supporting Spanish enhances accessibility.
    *   Competitive Performance: The model achieves promising results in benchmarks, placing it among the better open-source options.
    *   Public Availability:  This is a significant contribution that fosters further research and development.
    *   Strong Technical Details:  The paper provides sufficient detail on the architecture and training process.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited depth on hybrid attention mechanism impact:  While mentioned, there's a lack of thorough analysis of exactly how much performance improvement is gained from hybrid attention (vs. full global).  More ablation studies would be valuable.
    *   Missing Abalation studies to compare the new architecture to the previous one.
    *   Lack of novelties: most of the techniques used are based on previous research.
    *   The paper does not explore the safety or bias of the model.
    *   Limited quantitative discussion of training procedure and the effect of the ratio of reasoning mode data on model outputs.
    *   Lack of detailed cost and performance for different tasks.

* **Potential Influence:** EXAONE 4.0 has the potential to influence research in several areas:
    *   **AI Agents:** Providing a capable and accessible model for agent development.
    *   **Multilingual NLP:** Contributing to the advancement of Spanish language models.
    *   **Efficient Architectures:**  Potentially inspiring further research into hybrid attention mechanisms and optimization techniques.
    *   **Applied LLMs:** Serving as a solid foundation for real-world applications in various domains.

* **Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper.

*   It's a solid engineering contribution. The construction of a competitive LLM and its public release is valuable to the research community.
*   The architectural modifications (hybrid attention, layer norm) are sensible but not radically innovative.  More detailed ablations could have elevated the paper.
*   The practical focus on combining usability and reasoning and including agentic tool use features is a definite strength and demonstrates thoughtful design.
*   Overall, the paper represents a significant effort in applying and refining existing techniques rather than presenting a revolutionary breakthrough.

### KisMATH: Do LLMs Have Knowledge of Implicit Structures in Mathematical Reasoning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11408v1)
- **Authors**: Soumadeep Saha, Akshay Chaturvedi, Saptarshi Saha, Utpal Garain, Nicholas Asher
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thought traces have been shown to improve performance of large language models in a plethora of reasoning tasks, yet there is no consensus on the mechanism through which this performance boost is achieved. To shed more light on this, we introduce Causal CoT Graphs (CCGs), which are directed acyclic graphs automatically extracted from reasoning traces that model fine-grained causal dependencies in the language model output. A collection of $1671$ mathematical reasoning problems from MATH500, GSM8K and AIME, and their associated CCGs are compiled into our dataset -- \textbf{KisMATH}. Our detailed empirical analysis with 15 open-weight LLMs shows that (i) reasoning nodes in the CCG are mediators for the final answer, a condition necessary for reasoning; and (ii) LLMs emphasise reasoning paths given by the CCG, indicating that models internally realise structures akin to our graphs. KisMATH enables controlled, graph-aligned interventions and opens up avenues for further investigation into the role of chain-of-thought in LLM reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "KisMATH: Do LLMs Have Knowledge of Implicit Structures in Mathematical Reasoning?" introduces a novel dataset and methodology for analyzing how Large Language Models (LLMs) perform mathematical reasoning.  The authors propose Causal CoT Graphs (CCGs), which are automatically extracted directed acyclic graphs that model causal dependencies between mathematical expressions within LLM-generated chain-of-thought (CoT) traces. The authors create KisMATH, a dataset comprised of 1671 math problems from GSM8K, MATH500, and AIME, paired with LLM-generated solutions and their corresponding CCGs. They analyze a wide range of open-weight LLMs and find that (1) reasoning nodes within the CCG act as mediators between questions and answers (necessary for reasoning), and (2) LLMs emphasize reasoning paths defined by the CCG, suggesting an internal realization of these structures.  The KisMATH dataset is designed to facilitate controlled, graph-aligned interventions for deeper understanding of the role of CoT in LLM reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:

    *   **CCG Extraction:** The automated method for extracting causal graphs from CoT traces is a significant contribution. While manual annotation of such graphs exists, the scalability and automation presented here are new and valuable. This automation facilitates analysis on a much larger scale than previously possible.

    *   **KisMATH Dataset:** The creation of a dataset that explicitly links mathematical problems, LLM solutions, and causal graphs derived from CoT traces is a unique and valuable resource for the research community.

    *   **Graph-aligned interventions:** The ability to perform interventions based on the causal graph structure is a powerful tool for analyzing LLM reasoning that moves beyond stochastic perturbations.

*   **Significance:** The findings contribute to the ongoing debate about how CoT prompting improves LLM performance. The results provide evidence that LLMs recognize and emphasize relationships between intermediate steps that are defined in the CCGs.
    *   **Mediator Analysis:** Demonstrating that the reasoning nodes are effective mediators between questions and answers is important evidence towards the LLM implementing reasoning processes, rather than just retrieving solutions.

    *   **Implicit Structure:** Evidence that the LLM implicitly models a causal structure similar to CCGs further helps bridge the gap between 'retrieval' and actual reasoning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined methodology for extracting CCGs and performing analyses, including appropriate statistical tests (Kolmogorov-Smirnov test) to validate the findings.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments involve a broad range of LLMs, which increases the generalizability of the results. The results are consistent across different model architectures and sizes.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology, experiments, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifications:** The CCG extraction process involves simplifications and assumptions (e.g., defining matches based on exact strings or parse tree overlap) that may not perfectly capture the true causal relationships. Furthermore, the authors prune the graph, removing any isolated nodes. While this is done to maintain a connected graph, it could ignore some possible aspects of the trace. The model's implementation of causal links could be much more intricate than what is captured in the graph.
    *   **Definition of the match** The definition of mathcing of nodes appears to rely on shallow properties of the traces, such as matching for expressions. More semantic definitions based on math are possible.

    *   **Incomplete Explanation of "Bell"-shaped behavior:** The paper identifies "bell"-shaped and "exponential"-shaped behavior. While they provide an explanation about what to expect with either behavior, the origin of these behaviors is not fully explained.

    *   **Scope:** While the paper analyzes mathematical reasoning, the generalizability of the findings to other reasoning tasks is not explicitly addressed and warrants further investigation.
*   **Potential Impact:** The KisMATH dataset and CCG analysis methodology have the potential to be widely adopted by the research community for analyzing LLM reasoning. The findings could also inform the development of better LLMs by suggesting ways to explicitly encode causal structures into models.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**
The paper demonstrates strong novelty in its methodology and provides insightful evidence suggesting that LLMs are sensitive to the structure within CoT traces. The KisMATH dataset represents a significant contribution to the field that enables controlled, graph-based interventions in LLM reasoning. However, the simplifying assumptions in the CCG extraction and the incomplete discussion of the "bell"-shaped behavior detract somewhat from the paper's overall impact. While the analysis is thorough, broader application domains should be explored in future work. The score reflects both the strengths and the limitations of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quantifying the Energy Consumption and Carbon Emissions of LLM Inference via Simulations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11417v1)
- **Authors**: Miray Özcan, Philipp Wiesner, Philipp Weiß, Odej Kao
- **Abstract**: The environmental impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) is rising significantly, with inference now accounting for more than half of their total lifecycle carbon emissions. However, existing simulation frameworks, which are increasingly used to determine efficient LLM deployments, lack any concept of power and, therefore, cannot accurately estimate inference-related emissions. We present a simulation framework to assess the energy and carbon implications of LLM inference under varying deployment setups. First, we extend a high-fidelity LLM inference simulator with a GPU power model that estimates power consumption based on utilization metrics, enabling analysis across configurations like batch size, sequence length, and model parallelism. Second, we integrate simulation outputs into an energy system co-simulation environment to quantify carbon emissions under specific grid conditions and explore the potential of carbon-aware scheduling. Through scenario-based analysis, our framework reveals how inference parameters affect energy demand and carbon footprint, demonstrates a renewable offset potential of up to 69.2% in an illustrative deployment case, and provides a foundation for future carbon-aware inference infrastructure design.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a simulation framework designed to quantify the energy consumption and carbon emissions of Large Language Model (LLM) inference.  It extends an existing LLM inference simulator (Vidur) with a GPU power model that estimates power consumption based on utilization metrics like Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU).  This extended simulator is then integrated with an energy system co-simulation environment (Vessim) to model carbon emissions under specific grid conditions, including renewable energy availability and carbon intensity.  The authors use this framework to explore how inference parameters (batch size, sequence length, model parallelism) affect energy demand and carbon footprint, demonstrating the potential of renewable offset and laying the groundwork for carbon-aware inference infrastructure design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the **integration of existing tools and the specific application of MFU as a power proxy for LLM inference**. While tools like Vidur and Vessim exist, their combined use, particularly with the addition of a GPU power model tuned for LLM inference (even if a simplified one), is a valuable contribution. The use of MFU is a clever adaptation, given the limitations of standard GPU utilization metrics in accurately reflecting workload intensity, especially in memory-bound inference scenarios. However, the power model itself is relatively simple and based on published specifications rather than real-time power measurements, limiting its accuracy.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a timely and increasingly important problem: the environmental impact of LLM inference.  By providing a simulation framework, the authors enable researchers and practitioners to explore energy-performance trade-offs *before* deploying LLMs, which is valuable for designing more sustainable systems. The demonstrated potential for renewable offsets is significant and motivates further investigation. The framework's ability to model carbon emissions under dynamic grid conditions is a crucial feature, as it allows for exploring carbon-aware scheduling strategies. The use case showing that performance optimized configurations are not always the most efficient from an energy perspective highlights a crucial point and strengthens the importance of the paper. The paper makes a good case for how to integrate the disparate pieces to get the proposed result.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a critical problem:** The energy consumption and carbon emissions of LLM inference are significant and growing.
*   **Provides a practical framework:** The integration of Vidur and Vessim offers a concrete tool for exploring energy-aware LLM deployment.
*   **Adapts MFU effectively:** The use of MFU as a proxy for GPU power draw in LLM inference is a practical solution to the limitations of standard utilization metrics.
*   **Demonstrates potential benefits:** The case study highlights the potential for renewable energy offsets and the importance of considering grid conditions.
*   **Clearly articulated limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their power model and the need for further validation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplified power model:** The GPU power model is relatively simple and calibrated using public specifications rather than real-time power measurements, limiting its accuracy. The decision not to fit the 0.7 exponent to data (due to the lack of available power data) is a major weakness.
*   **Limited validation:** The lack of direct validation against empirical power data is a significant weakness.
*   **Exclusion of other energy considerations:** The model excludes CPU, memory, and cooling energy, which can be substantial in real-world deployments.
*   **Assumptions and simplifications:** several assumptions are made in the energy modelling.
*   **Limited scope of experiments:** The evaluation could be more comprehensive, exploring a wider range of models, hardware configurations, and grid conditions.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   **Encouraging the development of more sophisticated energy models for LLM inference.**
*   **Motivating the exploration of carbon-aware scheduling strategies for LLM deployments.**
*   **Raising awareness of the energy and carbon implications of different LLM inference parameters.**
*   **Providing a foundation for future research on sustainable LLM infrastructure design.**

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by integrating existing tools and proposing a framework for analyzing carbon-aware inference. However, the simplified power model and lack of comprehensive validation limit its impact. While the use of MFU as a power proxy is a strength, the need to fit the power exponent to empiricial results for validation is critical. Its ultimate influence will depend on how quickly this framework is picked up and extended with more sophisticated modelling and validation. The scope and experimental validation has also been limited.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Reasoning Strategies in Large Language Models: Can They Follow, Prefer, and Optimize?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11423v1)
- **Authors**: Yanjian Zhang, Guillaume Wisniewski, Nadi Tomeh, Thierry Charnois
- **Abstract**: Human reasoning involves different strategies, each suited to specific problems. Prior work shows that large language model (LLMs) tend to favor a single reasoning strategy, potentially limiting their effectiveness in diverse reasoning challenges. In this work, we investigate whether prompting can control LLMs reasoning strategies and assess its impact on logical problem-solving. While our experiments show that no single strategy consistently improves accuracy, performance could be enhanced if models could adaptively choose the optimal strategy. We propose methods to guide LLMs in strategy selection, highlighting new ways to refine their reasoning abilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reasoning Strategies in Large Language Models: Can They Follow, Prefer, and Optimize?"

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to adopt and utilize different reasoning strategies, mimicking human problem-solving. It explores whether prompting can control LLMs to follow specific strategies (Supposition Following, Chain Construction, Compound Reasoning, and Concatenation), assesses the effectiveness of each strategy, and proposes methods for adaptive strategy selection through ensemble methods. The study reveals that no single strategy consistently dominates, but performance can be enhanced by intelligently combining different strategies. The paper proposes several ensemble-based strategy selection mechanisms, including majority voting, confidence-based selection, and a model-based verifier, demonstrating improvements over single-strategy prompting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of multiple, human-inspired reasoning strategies within a single LLM using carefully designed prompts. While prior work identified strategy preferences in LLMs, this study goes further by explicitly controlling those strategies and investigating adaptive selection. The introduction of ensemble methods tailored for reasoning strategies, particularly the model-based verifier, offers a novel approach to improving LLM performance. However, the reasoning strategies themselves are not entirely novel; they are inspired by human cognitive science and, to some extent, reflected in existing prompt engineering techniques (e.g., CoT can be seen as a form of chain construction). The ensemble selection methods, while adapted for this context, are broadly based on standard machine learning practices.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for how we approach prompt engineering and LLM reasoning. The demonstration that LLMs can be steered into specific reasoning modes, even without fine-tuning, broadens the toolkit for developers aiming to tackle complex tasks. The observation that no single strategy is universally optimal underscores the limitations of relying on a single approach and highlights the potential benefits of adaptive strategy selection. The proposed ensemble methods offer a practical way to improve performance by leveraging the strengths of different reasoning styles. The finding that explicitly specifying a strategy in the prompt doesn't necessarily improve performance compared to allowing the model to choose its strategy highlights the model's limitations in selecting the best approach autonomously and suggests that more sophisticated meta-reasoning or context understanding is required.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Exploration:** The paper provides a well-structured and thorough analysis of different reasoning strategies and selection methods.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The experimental setup, prompt design, and evaluation metrics are clearly defined, making the results reproducible.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The proposed ensemble methods are relatively easy to implement and can be applied to existing LLMs without requiring retraining.
    *   **Insightful Findings:** The paper uncovers important insights about LLM reasoning, strategy preferences, and the potential for adaptive problem-solving.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on logical deduction problems, which may not generalize to all types of reasoning tasks. While the focus is on two datasets that are standard in the field, these do cover a relatively narrow set of problems.
    *   **Performance Gap:** Despite the improvements achieved through ensemble methods, a significant gap remains between the achieved accuracy and the oracle performance, suggesting that further improvements are possible.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The model-based verifier, while effective, adds a significant computational overhead due to the additional decoding steps. The practicality of the verifier is thus somewhat limited by the complexity of implementation and computational cost.
    *   **Verifier Quality:** The performance of the verifier model itself influences the outcome. A more robust and nuanced verifier could further enhance performance.
    *   **Simplistic Reasoning Strategies:** While the reasoning strategies are inspired by human cognition, they represent somewhat simplified versions. More complex and nuanced strategies could be investigated in future work.
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The effectiveness of different ensemble selection methods appears to be dependent on the specific dataset, requiring careful tuning and selection for optimal results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in prompt engineering, adaptive reasoning, and LLM evaluation. It provides a strong foundation for developing more sophisticated reasoning strategies and selection mechanisms, potentially leading to more robust and human-like problem-solving capabilities in LLMs. This work could also motivate researchers to explore the underlying cognitive biases that influence LLM reasoning and to develop methods for mitigating those biases.

Overall, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM research. While it has some limitations, its systematic approach, insightful findings, and practical implications make it a significant step towards developing more versatile and adaptive reasoning capabilities in these models. The paper opens up new avenues for future research in prompt engineering and ensemble selection methods.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant investigation into the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. While the study leverages existing strategies, the systematic exploration and integration of prompt-controlled reasoning strategies and ensemble methods is a valuable contribution. Despite the limited scope and challenges related to verifier quality and computational cost, the insights and practical implications make this a noteworthy paper that has the potential to influence future research directions. The performance gap between the results and the oracle performance suggests that further enhancements are possible, which supports a strong, though not exceptional, score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Implementing Adaptations for Vision AutoRegressive Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11441v1)
- **Authors**: Kaif Shaikh, Antoni Kowalczuk, Franziska Boenisch, Adam Dziedzic
- **Abstract**: Vision AutoRegressive model (VAR) was recently introduced as an alternative to Diffusion Models (DMs) in image generation domain. In this work we focus on its adaptations, which aim to fine-tune pre-trained models to perform specific downstream tasks, like medical data generation. While for DMs there exist many techniques, adaptations for VAR remain underexplored. Similarly, differentially private (DP) adaptations-ones that aim to preserve privacy of the adaptation data-have been extensively studied for DMs, while VAR lacks such solutions. In our work, we implement and benchmark many strategies for VAR, and compare them to state-of-the-art DM adaptation strategies. We observe that VAR outperforms DMs for non-DP adaptations, however, the performance of DP suffers, which necessitates further research in private adaptations for VAR. Code is available at https://github.com/sprintml/finetuning_var_dp.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores and benchmarks adaptations of Vision AutoRegressive (VAR) models, a recent alternative to Diffusion Models (DMs) in image generation. It focuses on fine-tuning VAR models for specific downstream tasks, similar to medical data generation, and investigates differentially private (DP) adaptations to protect sensitive fine-tuning data. The authors implement and benchmark various fine-tuning strategies, comparing their performance with state-of-the-art DM adaptation methods. They find that VAR outperforms DMs in non-DP adaptation scenarios, but suffers in DP settings, highlighting a need for further research in private adaptations for VAR. The paper's contribution includes code release to facilitate further research and bridge the gap between DMs and VARs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its **early exploration of adaptation techniques for VAR models**, a relatively new image generation paradigm. While DM adaptation is well-studied, the application of similar techniques to VAR is underexplored, making this work a valuable first step. The implementation and benchmarking of DP adaptations for VAR is also a significant contribution, as it identifies a key area where VAR models currently lag behind DMs.

*   **Significance:** The paper has potential significance for the image generation community, especially researchers interested in VAR models. The findings on VAR's superior performance in non-DP settings compared to DMs warrant further investigation into VAR as a viable alternative. The identification of challenges in DP adaptations provides a clear direction for future research. The code release is a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners wanting to explore VAR adaptation techniques.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the gap in adaptation strategies for VAR models.
    *   **Comprehensive benchmarking:** The paper provides a comprehensive comparison of various fine-tuning techniques, including full fine-tuning, LoRA, and LayerNorm Tuning.
    *   **Practical implementation:** The code release with the implementations of the methods and benchmarking setup facilitates further research.
    *   **Identifies limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of DP adaptations for VAR and provides insights on why this occurs.
    *   **Addressing Technical Barriers**: The paper goes in depth to describe how Opacus caused issues with existing VAR code and how they were overcome.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of DP experimentation:**  DP experimentation is limited to only one dataset and focused on two PEFT strategies.
    *   **Lack of in-depth analysis of failure in DP settings:** While the paper identifies the poor performance of DP adaptations, a deeper dive into the underlying reasons for this failure would be beneficial. The paper mentions larger gradients but doesn't sufficiently explore how architectural choices impact DP performance.
    *   **Incremental novelty:** The adaptation techniques themselves (LoRA, LayerNorm tuning) are not novel, but their application and benchmarking in the VAR context is.
    *   **Limited evaluation of image quality:** While FID scores are reported, the paper lacks qualitative analysis of generated images, which would provide a more complete picture of the performance of each adaptation method.
    *   **VAR code is too complex for Opacus**: The current implementation does not support zero-shot or low VRAM training with Opacus so the evaluation is limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence researchers working on VAR models and their adaptation. It provides a strong foundation for future work on developing more efficient and privacy-preserving adaptation strategies for VAR models.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper makes a valuable initial contribution by exploring and benchmarking adaptation techniques for VAR models, its weaknesses in DP analysis and the incremental nature of the adaptation methods keep the score from being higher. The VAR and PEFT techniques themselves are established, and the paper mainly combines them and assesses the effects. The work is certainly impactful, but only as an initial point of research, it does a good job of identifying issues that should be solved.

### HUG-VAS: A Hierarchical NURBS-Based Generative Model for Aortic Geometry Synthesis and Controllable Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11474v1)
- **Authors**: Pan Du, Mingqi Xu, Xiaozhi Zhu, Jian-xun Wang
- **Abstract**: Accurate characterization of vascular geometry is essential for cardiovascular diagnosis and treatment planning. Traditional statistical shape modeling (SSM) methods rely on linear assumptions, limiting their expressivity and scalability to complex topologies such as multi-branch vascular structures. We introduce HUG-VAS, a Hierarchical NURBS Generative model for Vascular geometry Synthesis, which integrates NURBS surface parameterization with diffusion-based generative modeling to synthesize realistic, fine-grained aortic geometries. Trained with 21 patient-specific samples, HUG-VAS generates anatomically faithful aortas with supra-aortic branches, yielding biomarker distributions that closely match those of the original dataset. HUG-VAS adopts a hierarchical architecture comprising a denoising diffusion model that generates centerlines and a guided diffusion model that synthesizes radial profiles conditioned on those centerlines, thereby capturing two layers of anatomical variability. Critically, the framework supports zero-shot conditional generation from image-derived priors, enabling practical applications such as interactive semi-automatic segmentation, robust reconstruction under degraded imaging conditions, and implantable device optimization. To our knowledge, HUG-VAS is the first SSM framework to bridge image-derived priors with generative shape modeling via a unified integration of NURBS parameterization and hierarchical diffusion processes.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided paper "HUG-VAS: A Hierarchical NURBS-Based Generative Model for Aortic Geometry Synthesis and Controllable Editing." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HUG-VAS, a novel hierarchical generative model for synthesizing and editing aortic geometries. The model combines NURBS (Non-Uniform Rational B-Splines) parameterization with diffusion-based generative modeling.  HUG-VAS has two primary components: first, a denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) which generates the vessel centerline. Second, a classifier-free guided diffusion model then generates the radial profiles conditioned on the generated centerline. Trained on a relatively small dataset of 21 patient-specific aortas, HUG-VAS is shown to generate anatomically realistic aortas, including supra-aortic branches. It supports both unconditional generation from random noise and zero-shot conditional generation from image-derived priors (e.g., points, contours, surface patches). The paper demonstrates the application of HUG-VAS in interactive semi-automatic segmentation, robust reconstruction from degraded imaging, and surgical implant design optimization.  It emphasizes the benefits of the NURBS representation (editability, compactness) and the hierarchical approach (decoupled centerline and radial profile generation for realistic variability).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    *   **Integration of NURBS and Diffusion Models:** While both NURBS-based modeling and diffusion models exist separately, their integration for generative shape modeling of vascular structures appears to be novel. The NURBS representation allows for explicit geometric control and editability, which is not typically found in other generative methods.
    *   **Hierarchical Approach:** Decoupling the centerline and radial profile generation and using conditional diffusion models to reflect their relationship appears to be a significant improvement over methods that treat them deterministically. This allows for greater anatomical variability and more realistic synthetic data. This enables the model to express greater inter-patient morphological variability.
    *   **Zero-Shot Conditional Generation:** The ability to generate geometries based on image-derived priors (points, contours, surface patches) *without retraining* is a compelling feature that significantly enhances the practical utility of the model for tasks such as semi-automatic segmentation and reconstruction.

* **Significance:**
    *   **Improved Realism and Variability:** The hierarchical approach and NURBS parameterization could lead to more realistic and diverse synthetic datasets for training machine learning models in cardiovascular applications, especially where fine-grained geometric detail is important. It captures local surface smoothness and geometric precision, critical for large vessels like the ascending aorta.
    *   **Practical Applications:** The paper showcases a range of practical applications, including semi-automatic segmentation, robust reconstruction, and surgical implant design, which directly address limitations in current clinical workflows. The semi-automatic segmentation workflow may overcome the reliance on manual segmentation. Also, this model may enable the optimization of surgical interventions.
    *   **Bridging Generative Modeling and Image-Derived Priors:** HUG-VAS shows promise in bridging the gap between purely data-driven generative models and image data, allowing users to incorporate anatomical knowledge and constraints into the generation process.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The model is trained on only 21 patient-specific aortas. This is a relatively small dataset, which could limit the generalizability of the model to unseen anatomies or pathologies.
    *   **Fixed Topology:** The model assumes a fixed branching topology, which limits its applicability to more complex vascular structures with varying branching patterns.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper doesn't explicitly address computational cost, diffusion models can be computationally expensive for both training and inference.
    *   **Lack of Extensive Validation:** While the results are promising, further quantitative validation is needed to rigorously assess the accuracy and robustness of HUG-VAS in various clinical scenarios. For example, comparing its segmentation performance against existing state-of-the-art segmentation methods. It is unclear how generalizable it would be to other pathologies, because it was only trained on thoracic aneurysm cases.

* **Potential Influence:**
    *   HUG-VAS could significantly influence the development of generative models for medical image analysis and cardiovascular applications.
    *   The integration of NURBS and diffusion models could inspire new approaches for generating and editing complex 3D anatomical structures.
    *   The semi-automatic segmentation workflow could improve the efficiency and accuracy of cardiovascular modeling.

* **Rationale for Score:**

The paper has significant novelty and potential significance. The combination of NURBS and diffusion models, the hierarchical approach to centerline and radial profile generation, and the zero-shot conditional generation capabilities are all compelling features. The practical applications showcased are relevant and could address important limitations in current clinical workflows. However, the small dataset size, fixed topology assumption, and the need for more extensive validation temper the overall impact. The potential, however, is substantial. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate.

### AirLLM: Diffusion Policy-based Adaptive LoRA for Remote Fine-Tuning of LLM over the Air
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11515v1)
- **Authors**: Shiyi Yang, Xiaoxue Yu, Rongpeng Li, Jianhang Zhu, Zhifeng Zhao, Honggang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Operating Large Language Models (LLMs) on edge devices is increasingly challenged by limited communication bandwidth and strained computational and memory costs. Thus, cloud-assisted remote fine-tuning becomes indispensable. Nevertheless, existing Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) approaches typically employ fixed or heuristic rank configurations, and the subsequent over-the-air transmission of all LoRA parameters could be rather inefficient. To address this limitation, we develop AirLLM, a hierarchical diffusion policy framework for communication-aware LoRA adaptation. Specifically, AirLLM models the rank configuration as a structured action vector that spans all LoRA-inserted projections. To solve the underlying high-dimensional sequential decision-making problem, a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) agent generates coarse-grained decisions by jointly observing wireless states and linguistic complexity, which are then refined via Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models (DDIM) to produce high-resolution, task- and channel-adaptive rank vectors. The two modules are optimized alternatively, with the DDIM trained under the Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) paradigm to maintain alignment with PPO rewards. Experiments under varying signal-to-noise ratios demonstrate that AirLLM consistently enhances fine-tuning performance while significantly reducing transmission costs, highlighting the effectiveness of reinforcement-driven, diffusion-refined rank adaptation for scalable and efficient remote fine-tuning over the air.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AirLLM, a novel framework for remote fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs) in scenarios with limited communication bandwidth, specifically in edge-cloud collaborative learning setups. AirLLM leverages a hierarchical diffusion policy framework for communication-aware Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). It models LoRA rank configuration as a structured action vector and employs a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) agent to generate coarse-grained decisions based on wireless states and linguistic complexity. These decisions are then refined using Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models (DDIM) to produce task- and channel-adaptive rank vectors. The two modules (PPO and DDIM) are trained alternately. Experimental results demonstrate that AirLLM enhances fine-tuning performance and reduces transmission costs compared to existing methods.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

The paper presents a combination of techniques, integrating Reinforcement Learning (RL) with Diffusion Models (DM) for adaptive Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) in resource-constrained environments. While each component (LoRA, PPO, DDIM) is known, their integration within the specific context of remote LLM fine-tuning and the attention to channel dynamics contribute to the paper's novelty.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** Addresses a relevant practical challenge: efficient remote fine-tuning of LLMs in edge-cloud settings, considering communication constraints (bandwidth, SNR). This distinguishes it from PEFT approaches that focus solely on the training process without considering deployment constraints.
    *   **Technical Approach:** The hierarchical policy based on PPO and DDIM is a reasonable approach to tackle the high-dimensional action space of layer-wise rank allocation. The use of a diffusion model to refine PPO actions is a novel and interesting element. The classifier-free guidance (CFG) to maintain alignment with PPO rewards is well motivated.
    *   **Comprehensive MDP Formulation:**  The explicit Markov Decision Process (MDP) formulation, incorporating wireless channel characteristics, data complexity metrics, and a multi-objective reward function to balance accuracy and communication cost, is a significant contribution.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed and demonstrate the effectiveness of AirLLM compared to AdaLoRA and other baselines under varying signal-to-noise ratios. The study of different design choices for the diffusion model is valuable. The results demonstrate both parameter transmission savings and accuracy improvements, highlighting the benefit of the joint approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Technical Contribution:** While the combination is novel, the individual components (PPO, DDIM) are well-established. The paper builds upon existing methods without introducing groundbreaking theoretical advances. The improvement might be attributed to the carefully engineered combination of techniques, rather than a fundamentally new algorithm.
    *   **Complexity and Tuning:** The framework's complexity (integrating PPO, DDIM, and handling channel dynamics) might make it challenging to implement and tune in practice. The parameter sensitivity analysis is limited. More details on tuning procedures and hyperparameter selection would be helpful for reproducibility.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The experiments focus on the SST-2 dataset and a specific LLM (OPT-1.3B).  It is not clear how well the results would generalize to other tasks, datasets, or LLM architectures. Experiments with more diverse scenarios are needed to demonstrate robust performance.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:**  The scalability of the DDIM component with a high number of layers, even with modifications is concerning since U-Net complexity with sequence length increases.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more practical and efficient LLM deployment strategies, particularly in resource-constrained edge computing environments. It provides a valuable case study on how RL and DMs can be combined to address complex optimization problems in machine learning. The emphasis on communication-aware adaptation is crucial for real-world LLM deployments.

Overall, the paper presents a well-motivated and executed study on a relevant problem. The integration of RL and diffusion models for adaptive LoRA is a novel contribution with clear practical implications. The major drawback is the incremental nature of the technical contribution, building upon existing methods rather than introducing fundamentally new theory or algorithms.
